3. Ivan IV (the Terrible), the First Tsar (1530-1584) 

NOTE: The title "terrible" (or "dreadful") is an incorrect translation of the Russian word "grozny." Its real rendition should be "awe-inspiring," "great," "wonderful" or "very serious,"

Ivan IV was but three years old when his father, Vasili, died in 1533, and his mother, Elena Glinski, assumed the regency. The country was in a state of great confusion. Not only were the princes constantly quarreling as usual, but the boyars had taken a hand in the struggle for power. After the death of Vasili, the government fell into the hands of these boyars and passed from one prominent family to another, the exchange of authority being accompanied by atrocities and executions, Thus Ivan grew up in an atmosphere of dissension, cruelty and murder, which in addition to his negative and unsystematic education, predisposed him to his later abnormal brutality, especially toward the boyars, whom he hated with particular bitterness, deeming them responsible for the disrupted state of the country. In estimating his character and policies, we must bear in mind also that he was a true child of his generation, a product of the Mongolian period when self interest, coupled with indifference to human suffering, had been the rule. Two hundred thirty-seven years of Tartar dominance were necessary to produce a man like Ivan the Terrible,* who would represent the culmination of barbarity. Yet strange as it may seem, he was the right man at the right time. He had sound judgment and vision and looked forward to a strong, united, mighty state under an autocratic monarchy, different from any that existed in 

Europe at that time.

He began his career in 1547 at the age of seventeen when he married and assumed the new title of "tsar," which was to be borne by all future emperors. first he attempted to deal diplomatically with the boyars, who thought to wrest his authority from him. He tried to win their support by concessions, now to one group, now to another, but these efforts on his part were interpreted as weakness by his opponents, who selfishly and blindly sought their own private interests at a time when Russia's very existence was threatened by outside foes. The most dangerous of these were Poland and Lithuania, which by this time were united under a single king, Stephen Batory (1576-86), one of the most able leaders of his time.

Toward the end of 1564, Ivan adopted a course of action which was to bring him absolute authority and make it possible for him to crush the boyars. He suddenly and secretly left Moscow and took up his residence in the village of Alexandrovskaya Sloboda, near the Trinity Monastery. From here he dispatched messengers to Moscow with the announcement that he had abdicated because of the treachery of the boyars. 

At this time Ivan's unpopularity with the nobility did not influence to a great extent In *Ivan was married seven times, but left only two young sons at his death. 

1580 in a fit of fury he killed his eldest son, which gives confirmation to the general belief that he suffered from insanity. These are the names of his wives: 

Anastasia (Romanov), Maria (Circasian), Martha, Anna, Anna Vasilchikuf, and Maria

Nagaya. He also desired to marry Queen Elizabeth of England and later tried vainly to wed Lady Mary Hastings. **It is extremely doubtful whether the title "tsar" (also Zarr, Czar, Czarr,

Ksar, Tsar) is derived from the Latin "Caesar" as is usually stated. The Tartar princes of Kazan were also called "tsars," See S. Graham, Ivan the Terrible, Yale 

University Press, 1933, pp. 39, 125-126.

his repute with the other classes; even the clergy accorded him a measure of respect. 

Moreover he had to his credit accomplishments which were popularly regarded as important. For example in 1552 he had overthrown Tartar control in the kingdom of 

Kazan and had taken possession of this territory which served as a gateway to further Russian expansion in the Far East. In 1555 he had forced Siberia to recognize the supremacy of Moscow, although the actual annexation of Siberia did not take place until 1584 after Yermak had led a Cossack expeditionary force against the region. 

In 1556 Ivan completed his conquest of Astrakhan. Hence his "abdication" speedily brought terrified entreaties from the civil and ecclestiastical population begging him not to forsake them, but to continue to rule as their tsar. Thereupon Ivan laid down the conditions of his "return" and forthwith instituted a regime marked by great cruelty and atrocity. Since his prime purpose was to destroy completely the hated boyar class, he demanded absolute and unquestioned authority to deal with the "traitors" as he saw fit. He further demanded that he be allowed to create a private guard for himself, which came to be known as "Oprichina." The word "Oprichina," which was coined by 

Ivan IV, means "separate" or "private" household or court. The members of the "Oprichina," about 6,000 in number, were reduced or elevated to the rank of monks "wearing black cassocks over sable necklets and cloth of gold." This was Ivan's most powerful agent in his policy of extermination. For its members he chose young men without regard for rank or station, taking care only that they should be fitted for his purpose. 

Naturally none of the "traitorous" boyars were included. His organization having been cample ted, he gave it license to slay and drive out the boyars, indicating that their lands would be awarded to members of the "Oprichina." 

Thereupon followed an "open season" for boyars, who had lost all protection of life and property.

One method of execution, much in favor with Ivan, consisted in inviting to dinner a group of boyars, who were hostile to him. When they had been reduced to a semi-comatose state by gluttony and drinkenness, the waiting "Oprichina" would suddenly fall upon them and indulge in wholesale decapitation. The country at large, while not exactly approving of Ivan's course of procedure, regarded it with more or less indifference, since the policies of the boyars, had brought extreme hardships to the people. Ivan himself, who was firmly convinced that Russia's future demanded the destruction of this class, continued his ruthlessness until he had attained his object. The boyars who managed to remain alive either fled from the country or were rendered impotent by being stripped of all wealth and position. 

The old system having been destroyed, Ivan established a new group of courtiers known as the dvorians. They dressed in black, called themselves brothers and adopted a life which ranged from the most devout religious observances to the wildest debauchery. From this group developed a new landowning, politically powerful class, the pomiestchiki, who earned their wealth and preferment by service for the Tsar. 

This group continued to be a factor to be reckoned with as late as the middle of the nineteenth century. Although Russia had virtually thrown off Tartar control, the Khans were still powerful enough to threaten seriously the peace and security of the country. In 1571, Devlet Girei, Khan of Crimea, conducted a raid into the interior of Russia, sacking and burning Moscow. The following year, he made another raid, and only the united forces of all Russia were able to drive him off. Following this invasion, Ivan altered his policy somewhat. Realizing that his government would be powerless in case of attack unless he could count upon the support of the entire population, he began to restore to the old nobility some of their lands and honors and to give his attention to the development of a settled middle class. His procedure, particularly his

I AND GOO 

bys C3

concessions to the nobility, met with much opposition on the part of the "Oprichina," despite the fact that he did not disregard their interests. The organization, however, began to decline in importance, having had almost absolute power for seven years. 

It was in Ivan's reign that the foundation was laid for the institution of serfdom. No matter who the various landowners in Russia had been, the peasants were the people who lived on the land and cultivated the soil. Up to the time of the pomiestchiki, they were entirely free and were in the habit of moving from one estate to another in accordance with their economic needs and desires. The pomiestchiki found that this migration was distinctly disadvantageous to the landowners and secured legislation for its limitation. Beginning with 1581, certain years were designated as "prohibited," and the peasant had to remain on a given estate during such a year. 

It was but a step, then, to make all years "prohibited," and the peasant became "attached" to the soil without freedom to leave. Thus the formerly free peasants were reduced to the status of serfs. 

After Ivan's death, the state of Moscow ordered a census and land survey. 

The information collected gave evidence, that by this time serfdom had become wellestablished, for in addition to legislation against migration, the peasants had fallen into debt to the landowners, which was yet another obstacle which prevented them from leaving the land. The peasants, therefore, were registered as belonging to the land. They were not, however, slaves in the sense that they were personal property of the landowner for if the land was sold, they were transferred wi th it. 

In addition to the serfs, there were, however, house servants, or Kholopi, who belonged to the owner and hence were true slaves. 

Ivan's policy of ferocity did much to hasten the formation of the Cossack states, if it did not actually create them. These states first came into existence in this wise. In the fourteenth century, the Ukraine was conquered by the Lithuanian forces under Prince Gedimin and became entirely separated from the north and from Tartar jurisdiction. By the middle of the fifteenth century Russians and even Tartars, who found Tartar or Lithuanian control entirely too unbearable, would seek homes in border territories by the southern steppes where they enjoyed a high degree of independence. One of their most profitable occupations consisted in robbing caravans.

These freebooters were called Cossacks by Turks and Tartars alike. The Turkish word for robber is Kazak. The same word is found in the Tartar language, but means a "light-armed warrior." In Manchuria, Cradle of Conflict (Macmillan 1932, p. 106),

Owen Lattimore says "The name Cossack was borrowed by the Russians from the Nomadic 

Qazaqs of southwestern Siberia."

When the oppression of Ivan the Terrible became excessive, many persons, especially of the lower classes, fled to this Cossack "land of the free and the home of the brave." In time this heterogeneous population established two states: the 

Host of the Don in southeastern Russia and the Zaporog Host in southwestern Russia.* *The literal meaning of "Zaporog" is "beyond the Cataracts" and refers to those of the Dnieper River. It is interesting to note that the Soviet Government as part of the Five Year Plan, constructed in the neighborhood of these cataracts the modern Dnieprostroy, the largest hydroelectric plant in Europe. The following book gives a vivid portrayal of the Zaporog Cossacks:- Taras Bulba, a short historical novel by Gogol. The Cossacks and Hadji Murad by Leo Tolstoy are interesting, fictiona accounts of Cossack life in general. For a description of Cossack life before and during the Great War and the Civil War in Russia, see The Silent Don, by Sholokhov. 

See also, The Cossacks by Maurice Hindus (N.Y., 1945).

5.

Serious The government of these states was quite democratic. All citizens were regarded as equal, and all property was held in common. At the head of the state was the Ataman or Hetman, elected by universal suffrage and in possession of strictly limited authority. Judicial power resided in the popular council or the "Circle." offenses were punished by drowning. A peculiar feature of the government was the imposition of celibacy upon members of the ruling class. The states were completely indifferent in their allegiance to their "Mother" land, fighting either for or against Russia as it suited their humor and interests. Their service to Christian civilization was rendered unconsciously, but was none the less effective. It consisted in their acting as buffer states to check further Mohammedan encroachment. These rude 

Cossacks of mixed antecedents were fully as fierce as the Asiatic invaders and kept the latter from completely overrunning Europe. Since fighting and depredation occupied most of their time, as a matter of course the Cossacks developed a system of military training and became adept in a kind of brutal warfare. Compulsory military service began when a youth reached the age of eighteen and entered the stanitsa. It continued for seventeen years, twelve of which were spent in active service, In addition to active service, there were two other types or classes of service, that is, on furlough with arms and horses and on furlough with arms but not with horses. The soldiers were equipped and clothed by the army or "host" from revenue granted by the government. It follows that a powerful and efficient military organization developed. At the beginning of World War I their forces numbered about 3,000,000 with 300,000 ready for immediate active service. 

They were divided into eleven hosts or corps, namely, Don, Kuban, Terek, Astrakhan, Orenburg, Ural, Siberia, Semiryetchenska, Transbakalia, Amur, and Ussuri.

An event of prime importance in the reign of Ivan the Terrible was the establishment of trade relations with the English, although Ivan himself had little to do with it. In 1553 the only remaining ship, Bonaventure, 160 tons, of an ill-fated 

English fleet, under the leadership of Sir Hugh Willoughy, which was seeking a passage to the Orient, drifted to the mouth of the northern Dvina. Moscow welcomed its captain, Richard Chancellor, and invited him to return. This he did as an official representative of the English Government, with power to negotiate for trade relations. Two years later the negotiations thus begun, were confirmed by a Russian embassy to England. Queen Mary of England, in February 1555, granted a charter to the "Russia Company." In the summer of the same year Richard Chancellor, captain of the Bonaventure, was drowned. 

Perhaps the greatest achievement of Ivan IV was his victory over the Mohammedan world which, at the time of his accession, extended as far west as the gates of Vienna. By his conquest of Kazan and Astrakhan he not only stemmed the tide of 

Mohammedan conquest, but reversed the trend. Not only did Russian expansion in Asia date from his reign, but Russia broke the ground for other European nations in this respect. 

4. The End of the Rurik Dynasty (1598) 

At his death, in 1584 (March, Kyril's Day), Ivan the Terrible left two sons, Fyodor (1584-1598), who became the second tsar of Russia, and Tsarevitch Dimitri, who was mysteriously murdered in 1591.* Indeed the entire reign of Tsar Fyodor is *Early historians accused Boris Godunov of instigating this murder in order to remove a troublesome obstacle to his usurpation of the throne. For a different point of view consult Boris Godunov by S. F. Platonov, Petrograd, 1921; Pushkin by 

Prince D. S. Mirsky, 1926, pp. 156-157; and Boris Godunof by Stephen Graham, Yale, 1933. 

For literature dealing with this event see: Pushkin, Boris Godunov; also Count 

A. L. Tolstoy's triology; Ivan the Terrible, Tsar Fyodor Ivanovitch, and Boris Godunov.

i:

shrouded in deep mystery, and no one knows for a certainty what dark deeds marked the closing years of the Rurik dynasty. 

Fyodor Ivanovitch was weak of will, fearful and lacking in initiative, the complete opposite of his father. Consequently it was easy for his brother-in-law, Boris 

Godunov, who had been the real ruler even in Ivan's last days, to direct the policy of the government.

Concerning the personality and character of this man, Boris Godunov, historians have come to no agreement. Some declare that he was a true patriot, who endeavored to render his country real service. Others maintain that he was influenced solely by motives of self-interest, his main purpose being to usurp the throne. It must be said that there is evidence to justify either opinion. There can be no doubt that as Prime Minister under Ivan, he was responsible for many a cruel edict, including the ukaz of 1581 by which serfdom was instituted. On the other hand, he exhibited ability as a statesman and not a few of his acts bear the stamp of true patriotism as it was understood in his time. In any event when Tsar Fyodor died childless on the eighth of January, 1598, thus bringing to an end the Rurik dynasty, Boris Godunov was elected tsar by the Zemsky Sobor, a popular assembly organized by 

Ivan IV in 1550. Like Ivan IV, Boris Godunov (1598-1605) recognized the importance of a strong middle class and tried to advance its interests. This policy, however, merely served to alienate the boyars and rich untitled nobility, while the middle class had not yet sufficient strength to take advantage of its opportunities and counteract the dissatisfaction not only of the upper classes, but also that of the other extreme of society, namely, the lower classes. Boris Godunov's policy in this respect and its results call to mind similar efforts and similar results on the part of Stolypin, Prime Minister under Nicholas II, 1906-1911. While the upper classes were enraged because of curtailment of their political power, the lower classes were suffering dire want. Hence the ruler had two problems to handle, one political, the other, economic. Boris Godunov did his best to solve both and made concessions and advances to all classes of society, without, however, gaining any support. This was not due solely to the fact that in endeavoring to satisfy one group, he estranged the others, but chiefly because he did not belong to the Rurik royal line. 

The people would tolerate a great deal from a weak or cruel, legitimate ruler, but when it came to enduring oppression and suffering from an elected tsar of Tartar extraction, that was an entirely different matter. That was a little too much even for those days.

The dissatisfied boyars finally declared void the election of the Zemsky Sobor by which Boris Godunov had become tsar. They announced that the real ruler was in Poland, thus again bringing foreign influence into Russian affairs. In the pretender, they claimed to have discovered the Prince Dimitri who, they said, had not been murdered in 1591 after all, same one else having fallen a victim in his place. The king of Poland and the Jesuits viewed the appearance of a pretender with delight, for they saw an opportunity to widen the sphere of both Polish and Catholic influence. Encouraged by the promise of support both within and without Russia, the pretender appeared in Moscow in 1605. Immediately insurrection broke out, and still another "Tsarevitch Dimitri" appeared, this time the candidate of the lower classes, who had risen under the leadership of an escaped slave, Bolotnikov. Hardly had this revolt been put down when the Cossacks led another uprising. In a short time Russia was embroiled in the most bitter civil war and anarchy, and hence an easy prey to external foes, especially the Poles and Swedes, who made haste to occupy Russian territory.

The invaders, however, pressed their advantage too far and brought about a reaction which led to their expulsion. During the Polish occupation of Moscow, the patriarch of that city, Hermorgen, refused to recognize foreign dominance, and

in punishment was starved to death by the enraged Poles. This, together with other atrocities, aroused a religious and nationalistic feeling among the Russians. A middle class merchant of Nizhni-Novgorod, K. Minin by name, took the initiative* in arousing his own class and bringing about a union with the nobility, led by Prince Dimitri Pozharsky, against the foreign encroachments. Aided by the more moderate of the Cossacks, they succeeded in driving out the Poles and in taking possession of Moscow in 1612. A few sporadic and minor uprisings still called for suppression, but in the course of another year these were checked and the country gradually returned to more normal conditions. In 1613 the Zemsky Sobor elected as tsar of Russia a young boyar, Michael Romanov, with whom the Romanov dynasty began. The "Time of Trouble," as the years 1605 to 1613 were called, had come to an end. *This indicates that in recognizing the important potentialities of the middle class, both Ivan IV and Boris Godunov exhibited sound judgment.

THE ROMANOV DYNASTY 

(1613-1917) 

From Tsar to Emperor (Michael to Peter I, 1613-1682) 10 

Reign of Tsar Michael Fyodorovitch (1613-1645). 

The Zemsky Sobor elected the new tsar without a dissenting vote, despite the fact that he was very young and not in particularly robust health. The problem which confronted him was difficult indeed, and would have tested the powers of a mature man; nevertheless, Tsar Michael approached his task with praiseworthy energy and good judgment. The insurrectionists of the "Time of Trouble"--Cossacks, peasants, and others--had produced a condition of chaotic confusion in the state and had been guilty of atrocities, which caused the deeds of Ivan the Terrible to pale into insignificance. 

The Tsar's first efforts, therefore, were directed toward the suppression of the activities of various brigands and outlaws. Scarcely had he succeeded in this, and in producing a degree of order in his distracted country when he had to cope with a yet more difficult problem, for the boyars resumed their old tactics of exploitation of the country for their private ends. Stooping to any despicable means of attaining their purpose, they were able to secure the most desirable "pork barrel" appointments, and the treasury became the prey of their systematic plundering. One of their most dastardly practices was their custom of demanding of the Tsarina that she use her influence to further their nefarious schemes. Failure on her part to comply endangereċ her career and even her life. Thus they secured by false charges the expulsion from the palace of Michael's first bride, while her successor was poisoned. It was the general knowledge of this atrocious procedure which caused the King of Denmark to refuse to permit bis niece to become Michael's wife. In

Fortunately an able adviser came to Michael's assistance in this crisis. In 

1619 his father, Philaret Romanov, was returned as an exchange prisoner after a nine years' captivity in Poland. He was immediately consecrated patriarch as successor to the martyred Hermogen, the office having remained vacant up to this time in anticipation of his return. His ecclesiastical duties, which he performed with great efficiency, did not prevent him from aiding his son in the most effectual manner. fact, although Michael retained the title of tsar, it was his father who, to all intents and purposes, was the real ruler. The country benefited enomously under the direction of this able statesman, who was well versed in all worldly, as well as clerical matters, and whose impelling and brilliant oratory swayed the people to his will. Under his guidance, Michael checked the depredations of the boyars, established internal order and set about adjusting Russian foreign relations. Russi

The Swedes and the Poles constituted the most serious menace to the national integrity of the country, the latter even laying claim to the throne of Moscow. was not yet strong enough to force her assailants to make a satisfactory peace. Thus the treaty which Michael was able to secure from the Swedes was far from favorable to his country. Such restitutions as were made to Russia for past encroachments were of distinctly minor importance, whereas her territorial loss cut her off entirely from the Black Sea, leaving her with Archangel as her sole port for direct water communication with the rest of Europe.

While a permanent agreement with the Poles had to be delayed until 1631-32, by which time Russia had grown strong enough to force recognition of her demands, Foland had become distinctly uneasy during the "Time of Trouble," for the conditions. 26

{ .. !...

prevalent in Russia threatened to spread to other territories, which offered fertile soil for the propagation of anarchy. Hence they were more than a little relieved by the restoration of order in the Russian state, and had no mind to see the internal stability of the country threatened by Poland. Thus a number of European governments, including that of the Sultan Osman of Turkey, offered Russia their assistance in checking Polish pretensions and likewise sought to establish political and commercial relations with her. While realizing that her neighbors were inspired in many instance: by quite ulterior motives, Russia recognized the importance of their support and expressed her appreciation thereof, without, however, permitting herself to be drawn into compromising entanglements. For example James I of England made a loan of 

20,000 rubles to Moscow. In return for this favor, John Merrik, English representative, demanded that Russia should grant to England free of duty two trade routes, one to Persia by way of the Volga and the other through Siberia to Hindustan. Such concessions would have utterly ruined Russia's own trade and hence were refused. At the same time the 20,000 rubles were promptly repaid.

One of the most beneficial results of Russia's relations with Western powers was the importation of foreign goods and the caning of a large number of foreigners. 

Michael encouraged foreign merchants and craftsmen to take up a residence in the country and did his best to stimulate foreign trade. Thus Western influence was considerably extended during his reign, and his subjects began to adopt some of the customs of their European neighbors and hence had more in common with them. 

European customs, however, met with equal favor. Thus the people were commanded to abstain from the use of tobacco, while snuff takers were discouraged to the extent of having their noses cut off.

It will be remembered that during the reign of Ivan the Terrible, the peasants lost their freedom and became "attached" to the soil, the length of time which they Wsre obliged to spend upon a given estate being gradually lengthened. Nevertheless, the landowners were not satisfied, but were continually clamoring for legislation which would deprive the wretched peasants of the modicum of liberty left to them and "attach" them permanently to the estates. The peasants, for their part, were immeasurably discontented with their hard lot and were constantly seeking some alleviation of their misery. In consequence, there was much ill-will and strife between them and the land owners, and fugitives from the estates increased in number. 

In order to relieve the situation, laws were passed during Michael's reign, which, however, were entirely in favor of the owners of the estates, and which established serfdom even more firmly than before. The term of "fixation" to the soil was extended from the five year period instituted by Boris Godunov to ten and even fifteen years, and the landowners were given the right to reclaim fugitive peasants. 

To sum up, Michael, during his reign, restored internal peace and established important foreign relations. He maintained an "open door" policy toward foreign trade and foreign tradesmen. He rescued Russia from a state of anarchy and secured for her the recognition of Europe as a power which must be respected. He developed a national feeling so that the people no longer regarded themselves as members of various principalities, but as Russians, natives of a united state with a central authority, emanating from Moscow. It was Ivan the Terrible who made from the principalities the state of Moscow and placed the Tsar above all princes, who had Some claim upon the throne or connection with the royal line. Michael, for his part, nade from the Moscow tsardom the entity which later developed into the Russian Empire. 

His most negative policy was the strengthening of serfdom which rendered still more acute the peasant problem, which was to became an increasing source of difficulty to the country.

Historians are wont to compare Michael's reign with the reign of Henry IV of The problems and difficulties, both internal and external, which confronted the two monarchs had many similarities. France. 2. 

Reign of Tsar Alexei Michailovitch (1645-1676). 

Even Ivan the Terrible had

Michael was succeeded by his sixteen year old son, Alexei. The most important event of the latter's reign was the Rascol, or Church Schism, through which two opposing groups developed in the church. Of these, the Old Believers vigorously opposed any changes in the existing creed and liturgy, whereas the Refomers, or Moderns, advocated certain ritualistic modifications. Heretofore Russia had concerned herself very little with religious controversy. 

To be sure, some faint echoes of the Protestant Reformation had reached the country. been sufficiently interested to make investigations concerning the new faith. The terrific altercations, however, which were rocking all other European nations, did not affect Russia to any marked extent. The problems, which arose as an aftermath of the Mongolian period, together with the serious disorders of the "Time of Trouble," had so occupied all classes of society that they had little time or inclination to engage in additional disputes. On July 23, 1652, however, Nikon, a churchman of distinctly modern tendencies, became patriarch in Moscow. Immediately he set to work to bring about ritualistic changes and reforms which he regarded as essential. Among other things, he wished the Russians to follow the Greek practice of joining three fingers in making the sign of the cross, thus symbolizing the Trinity. The Russians had been accustomed to join two fingers to symbolize the dual nature of Jesus Christ. He met with no little opposition on the part of both clergy and laity, who bitterly resented any innovations particularly if these seemed to indicate an imitation of Western practices. In their opinion, Moscow was the "Third Rome" and the Russian Church alone had retained Christianity in its original purity, even the Greek Church having deteriorated to a lamentable extent. Any changes, therefore, assumed in their eyes the aspect of disastrous retrogression. Nikon was determined, however, and was so successful that by 1654, the Church Council had approved his reforms and incorporated the same in church books and rituals. Then, incited by him, the state took a hand in the matter and began to persecute dissenters by torture, imprisonment and death at the stake. 

No measures were sufficiently severe, however, to bring conformity. For the Old 

Believers, the changes in the Church bore all the marks of a world catastrophe, and they stubbornly refused to relinquish their principles. It is recorded that more than 

20,000 of them voluntarily sought death by fire rather than accept the new forms of worship.

Being unwilling to participate in services following the new order, they eithe: dispensed with priests entirely or chose from among themselves men to act as their leaders, who unfortunately were often ignorant and untrained. Sometimes also they availed themselves of the services of priests, who came from southwestern Russia. 

These priests, by reason of their frequent contact with Roman Catholic Poles, had become very argumentative. They, as well as their untrained brethren, brought distinctly undesirable elements into the Russian priesthood. For a detailed description of the life of the Old Believers, see the novels In the Forests and On the Mountains by Petcherski (Melnikov). Having secured official adoption and governmental support of his reforms, Nikon could afford to disregard the resistance of the dissenters and turned his energies in another direction. His ambitions now caused him to reach out for temporal power, for he dreamt of a future in which the head of the Church should stand above

Esta

the tsar, in spiritual matters at least. His efforts to bring this about led to the first serious clash between Church and State in Russia and caused his own downfall. 

In 1666 he was deposed from his patriarchate and exiled. He died in 1681. 

The religious controversy gave rise to discussions, which embraced social concerns, as well as purely religious interests. Eventually two rather sharply opposed groups made their appearance, the members of which may be called the Slavophiles and Westernizers, respectively. The former were intensely national in their sentiments. They had much in common with the Old Believers of the religious controversy, inasmuch as they also maintained that pure Christianity was to be found in Russia alone and that Moscow was the "Third Rome." Further they maintained that the 

Russian people constituted a "New Israel," over which the tsar reigned as the sovereign of all Orthodoxy. As a matter of course, they did not favor innovations or influences of foreign origin but sought rather to develop and intensify that which was national and innate, either in spiritual or material concerns. The first real 

Slavophile was Macarius, metropolitan of Moscow, during the reign of Ivan the Terrible. 

He was the first to regard Moscow as the "Third Rome" and the tsar as the head of Orthodoxy. The Westernizers, on the other hand, welcomed foreign ideas much as their predecessors of the Mongolian period had welcomed foreign armies, They believed that Russia was behind other European countries, both in the field of thought and in material progress, and that, in consequence, the West had much to give her that was beneficial. Hence they favored the adoption and incorporation into national life of any elements which would contribute to national welfare and progress. They sedulously cultivated, therefore, all foreign contacts and endeavored to govern their lives in accordance with Western ideals. 

One potent source of Western influence much utilized by them, was to be found in colonies of foreigners, established within the country. While during the reign of Alexei, tradesmen of foreign extraction were compelled to confine their activities to the neighborhood of Archangel, the same restrictions were not imposed upon foreign intelligentsia. Many of the latter of German or Anglo-Saxon antecedents settled more or less permanently in Russia, particularly in the neighborhood of Moscow, from whence they contributed to the dissemination of reforms favored by the Westernizers. 

These exponents of the two opposing ideals for national betterment namely, the Slavophiles and the Westernizers, continued throughout an extended period to advocate their principles with the utmost energy. The respective movements gained such momentum that by the 19th century, they occupied a disproportionate amount of public thought and discussion, as is so ably revealed in the writings of Turgenev and Dostoyevsky.

While the various religionists and national idealists were consuming their energies in what were often mere hair-splitting controversies, the mass of the population was in dire need of a practical handling of serious economic and social problems. Unemployment had increased alarmingly, and the discontent engendered thereby constituted a serious menace to national peace and welfare. The bondage of the serfs had become so increasingly oppressive that they were raging in impotent misery. Among all orders of society, intense and dangerous class hatred prevailed. 

A contributory cause of this class feeling was the legislation, which Alexei, in imitation of his predecessors of the Rurik dynasty, had enacted with the intent of advancing the interests of the slowly developing middle class, or minor nobility. 

As a result both the upper nobility and the lower were equally enraged by discriminating laws, while the middle class was neither of sufficient size nor strength to take advantage of its privileges and assume a position of leadership.

1. ​16

The general and wide-spread dissatisfaction found an outlet among the lower classes in the years 1667 to 1671 in an uprising headed by Stepan (Stenka) Razin, the Russian Robin Hood. This man, who proclaimed himself an enemy of the rich and a brother of the poor, had none of the qualities of an able leader, who could inaugurate beneficial social reforms. He was merely a bandit at the head of a motley crew of robbers and murderers. He drew his first followers from the most indigent Cossacks, the "Naked," as they were called. With a force augmented by recruits from the dissatisfied villagers, he overran in a short time a great expanse of territory, plundering wherever the opportunity offered and murdering boyars, public officials and even members of the clergy. Finally his own Cossacks turned against him, and betrayed him to the authorities. After being put to torture, he was executed in 

Moscow in 1671, and the uprising came to an end. 

The following song is attributed to Razin, who is said to have left it as a "bequest" to his followers: "Bury me, brothers, between three roads,

The Kiev, and the Moscow, and the Murom, famed in story. 

At my feet fasten my horse; 

At my head set a life-bestowing cross; 

In my right arm place my keen sabre. 

Whoever passes by will stop; 

Before my life-bestowing cross will he utter a prayer; 

At the sight of my black steed will he be startled;

At the sight of my keen sword will he be terrified. 'Surely, this is a brigand who is buried here! 

A son of a brigand, the bold Stenko Razin'. (Ralston, Sh., Songs of the Russian People, p. 46).

The internal disorders of Alexei's reign were paralleled by external troubles. 

Extended and bitter wars with Poland and Lithuania over the possession of Little Russia, or the Ukraine, occupied many years. Heretofore the Western powers had always been the aggressors, and Russia had not been strong enough to check their encroachment: effectively. This time, however, she took the offensive and succeeded in acquiring control of much territory, which had been wrested from her in former years. This territory comprised Smolensk, Sieversk, Kiev and that part of the Ukraine, located on the left bank of the Dnieper. Out of the conflict with Poland there developed struggles with Sweden and Turkey, which were, however, of little importance. The combatants withdrew from the Swedish War (1656-1659) without gain or loss of territory on either side, A few skirmishes on the bank of the Dnieper constituted the whole of the war with the Turks. In 1681 Tsar Fyodor, son of Alexei, signed a treaty of peace with them. Alexei had died in 1676. 

3. Reign of Tsar Fyodor Alexeyevitch (1676-1682). 

Tsar Alexei left three sons and a daughter, Fyodor, Ivan, and Sophia, children of his first wife, Maria Miloslavskaia, and Peter, son of his second wife, Natalia Narishkina. Fyodor succeeded his father to the throne. He was a man lacking in capability and ambition and of so feeble a constitution that he survived Alexei by only six years, dying in 1682. After his death, a bitter feud broke out between two opposing court parties. One of these supported Ivan's claim to the throne, calling themselves Miloslavskys in honor of his mother. The other party was known as the 

Narishkins and demanded the accession of Peter on the ground that Ivan was incapacitat. ed. 

Supported by the patriarch, the boyars and a considerable number of the citizens, the Narishkins succeeded in gaining their point temporarily. The Miloslavskys,

however, stirred up the Streltzy,* who demanded that Ivan be given his rightful place in the succession and instituted a period of such confusion and bloodshed that a compromise became necessary. It was decided that the throne should be held jointly by Ivan and Peter, with their elder sister, Sophia, as regent. Thus governmental control was really in the hands of the Miloslavskys. Although Tsarevna Sophia (1682-1689) was a woman of much intelligence and ability, she was not a successful ruler; especially was her foreign policy disapproved by her subjects, despite the fact that in 1686 she succeeded in making a treaty of "perpetual" peace with Poland, securing by the terms of the same treaty an alliance with the Poles against the Turks.

She hated and feared her half-brother, Peter, recognizing in him a dangerous menace to the continuation of her authority and considered ways and means for his removal. He was not unaware of hor animosity and discovering that she was plotting against his life, he took measures to secure her overthrow. With the support of one faction of the Streltzy, he brought about a coup d'etat in 1689, which wrested the government from Sophia's hands. She was sent to a convent, where she was virtually a prisoner, and Peter assumed absolute control, although his brother, Ivan, lived until 1696. *As a protection for Moscow, Ivan the Terrible had organized several regiments of infantry, known as the Streltzy. This body became very powerful by reason of its good military organization and the special privileges granted to it. It eventually became so independent and arrogant that it was able to force its will upon the government, which did not dare to check its activities.

FROM REFORMER TO REFORMER (PETER I TO ALEXANDER II, 1682-1855) 1. Reign of Peter I, the Great, First Emperor (1682-1725). 

Peter was only seventeen years old when he made himself master of Russia, but had already attained mental and physical maturity. His superior intelligence had been manifest at an early age when he had rapidly mastered the subjects which constituted a liberal education of his day. His interests and abilities were not exclusively academic, however, for ships and ship-building had a great fascination for him. He also had a strong militaristic bent, which caused him as a very young boy to begin the organization of regiments among his youthful companions, which regiments later served him well.

His formal instruction was interrupted by the hostilities following the death of his brother, Fyodor. When Sophia became regent, she selected tutors for him, but his mother so strongly disapproved of them that she withdrew with him to the village of Preobrazhenskoye. This village was near the German Suburb, a rendezvous of all sorts and conditions of foreigners. The German Suburb was originally founded by some adventurous Englishmen and a group of soldiers from the army of the famous Wallenstein. Later 3000 Scotch refugees, who had fled after the imprisonment of 

Charles I, joined the community. As time went on, the population became exceedingly cosmopolitan. Since very few of the inhabitants could speak Russian, the Russians applied to all of them the name Nemetz (German) from Nemoi, meaning speechless. 

The Church regarded it as a resort of heretics and an abode of evil and tolerated its existence with difficulty. Patriarch Joachim, who died in 1690, left as a bequest to Peter the injunction not to associate with Lutherans, Calvinists, Catholics and like heretics but rather to banish them from the country and raze their places of worship to the ground. It was, nevertheless, much frequented by Russians who found stimulus in the varied intellectual contacts which it offered, or who sought relaxation from the restraints imposed by Russian conventionalists.

Peter visited the German Suburb constantly and made numerous friends and acquaintances among its inhabitants. Through his association with the philosophers, scholars and technicians of various nationalities and with Protestant missionaries of German and Dutch extraction, he became imbued with Western ideas and turned a critical eye upon conditions in his own country. He began to study Russian history and became convinced that in pre-Mongolian days, Russia had possessed a degree of culture and had reached a stage of development which would have enabled her easily to keep abreast of or even outstrip other European nations. He also saw that the 

Mongolian invasion and the long period of foreign bondage, which completely severed Russia from cultural contacts with the West, had resulted in a fearful retardation of her civilization; so that she was centuries behind her neighbors. In order to bridge the gulf which separated Russia from Western culture, he determined to effect by force a complete change in the customs, ideas and character of his people by the eradication of all relics of Mongolianism and the substitution of European civilization. Whereas the Tartars had transformed Russia, it was his purpose to reform her in accordance with Western ideals, toward which end he proceeded with relentless determination.

A. Internal Policy: 

In order to understand Peter's internal policy, we must bear in mind that his prime purpose was to change Russia from an isolated Orthodox Slavo-Mongolian nation 32

IT ܐܐ܂

into a Christian European nation.

Therefore his Europeanization of the country became in reality a Protestantization, and many of his reforms were accomplished at the expense of the Orthodox Church. As has been noted previously, during the 

Mongolian period, the Church incorporated within itself many Oriental features, the origin of which came to be forgotten in the course of centuries. Peter was keenly alive to this source of Oriental, and in his opinion, pernicious influence, and knew that it must be destroyed if his program of Europeanization was to succeed. 

Moreover he resented the efforts of the Church to secure temporal power and resolved to crush such pretensions without delay. Thus, though he joined with the Church in its persecution of the Old Believers, whom he regarded also as a troublesome obstacle to progress, he omitted nothing which would serve to injure ecclesiastical power and prestige and render its influence ineffective. In consequence his procedure was bitterly resented by all churchmen who voiced their indignation so emphatically that for many years he was referred to as an infidel, the "Anti-Christ," whose rule had wrought incomparable harm to his country. 

One of his most effective blows against the Church was the abolition of the office of patriarch in 1700.* There was no supreme ecclesiastical authority until 

1721 when Peter instituted a synod, but as this body could act only at his direction, he himself became thereby the actual head of the Church. He also turned against 

Moscow, the "Third Rome," the "Second Jerusalem," and robbed it of its glory by removing his capital to the new city of St. Petersburg, founded in 1703. To be sure, he had selected the site for this city because he considered that the capital needed a more strategic location than Moscow afforded, but he also had in mind the entire separation of the government from any possibility of Church control or influence. 

So determined was he to stamp out this influence that when he learned that, his son, Alexei, had promised the clergy to restore the capital and to reassert the authority of the Church upon his ascession, he caused the young man to be murdered (1718). 

By this time monasticism had become a well-established institution in the Church and exerted no little influence along educational lines. Peter, however, made it the object of ridicule upon all occasions, passed laws to eradicate it and deprived its members of any public offices, which they had secured. For example, the Department of Education had been in charge of an Orthodox monk; Peter dismissed him and gave the office to a Protestant pastor. 11 As a further "Church Reform," in 1721 he discarded the title of "tsar" because of its Oriental and ecclesiastical implication and substituted for it the Western title of "emperor. The people in general, however, continued to call their ruler the "tsar." In order to give this change especial significance, he selected as the day for, announcing it, November 4, the anniversary of the freeing of Moscow from the Poles in 1612. As a matter of course, he also changed the name "Grand 

States of the Russian Tsardom" to the "Empire of all the Russias" to conform with European usage. Already as early as 1708, he had reorganized the country into provinces in accordance with European principles of government. 

Second only to the Church Peter regarded the boyars as a reactionary and detrimental force. While he did not seek to annihilate them as did Ivan the Terrible, he effectively deprived them of all political and social power. In 1722 he published *Job of Rostov became the first Patriarch of Russia in 1586; was solemnly crowned, January 26, 1589; died 1605. The Patriarchate lasted for 110 years, and was abolished by Peter I. After the outbreak of the Russian Revolution in 1917, an attempt was made to revive the ecclesiastical institution but without success.

1.

a "Table of Ranks" by which it became known that the old aristocracy by right of birth had been abolished, and in its place had been established a new aristocracy, the dvorianstvo, whose titles were bestowed because of service to the government. In thus making a patent of nobility contingent upon the rendition of service to the state, Peter gave a powerful incentive to all classes to give such service. The "Table of Ranks" was abolished on December 10, 1917.

In the field of education he adopted a distinctly constructive policy and was responsible for much valuable progress. The Academy of Science, organized in imitation of a similar institution in Paris, was founded by him. It speedily attracted scientists and mathematicians in great number and contributed much to the advance along scientific and technical lines. Its most valuable accomplishments were the geographical survey of Siberia and the great Siberian expedition of 1733 to 1743. То assist the work of the Academy, Peter also caused books on technical subjects to be translated from various European languages. His instructions to the translators show that he had a true comprehension of what constitutes the art of translation. He admonished them to acquire a thorough understanding of the content and to translate the thought rather than mere words.

With the view to furthering education of a more popular character, Peter established the first public newspaper in 1703 and in the same year authorized the erection of the first public theatre, the Comedy House, at the Red Square. 

In his zeal to reform his subjects, Peter did not forbear to interfere with the minor details of their daily life. He commanded all of them to adopt European dress. He forced the men to shave their abundant beards and made the women abandon their Oriental seclusion. The people often objected strenuously, but were compelled in the end to submit. Despite his harsh rule, he won their respect and by his unaffected, ofttimes coarse behavior, he gained their liking because they felt that he was one of the "common people." His great height of six feet seven and his huge frame were also not without effect in arousing respect and inspiring fear. 

The clergy, the boyars, and Slavophiles in general bitterly censured his methods, citing his private life, which was indeed far from immaculate, as an evidence of his depravity, and characterizing him as the "Deformer" of Russia. 

He was, however, above all things, heart and soul a Russian, and the practices and customs which he opposed were not Russian at all but Oriental relics of the Mongolian period. 

The reforms, which he forced upon his people, contributed for the most part to their welfare and progress. His vision extended far beyond that of his countrymen. He had a great intellect and a great mission, and was most truly the Reformer of Russia. 

Unfortunately the institution of serfdom became more firmly established during his reign. His great building projects required much forced labor by the serfs, which caused control of them to became stricter and more harsh. The only mitigation in the lot of the serf was the introduction of a law forbidding the sale of individual slaves; henceforward "the family must be disposed of as a whole." 

Opposition to Peter's reforms was still reflected years later in the works of N. M. Karamzin (1766-1826), renowned Russian historian, who voiced the sentiment of the Slavophiles as follows: "We became citizens of the world, but ceased in same respects to be citizens of Russia." 

B. External Policy: 

General Plan. "We must progress as much as possible in the direction of 

Constantinople and India. He who can once get possession of these points is the real

ruler of the world.

With this view we must provoke constant quarrels--at one time with Turkey, and at another with Persia... "We must take away as much territory as possible from Sweden, which by history and fable belonged to Russia of old time, and contrive that they shall attack us first, so as to give us a pretext for their subjugation. With this object in view, we must keep Sweden in opposition to Denmark, and Denmark to Sweden and sedulously foster their mutual jealousies."

As the first step in his plan of expansion, he resolved to advance against the impregnable Turkish stronghold of Azov. A campaign of this nature necessitated an army drilled to the last degree of perfection in military tactics, and Peter had long been preparing such a force. The regiments which he had drilled as a boy in the village of Preobrazhenskoe were now seasoned, disciplined troops. Their long training produced valuable results for in 1695 the unconquerable Azov actually surrendered to them. During Michael's reign it had once been taken by the Cossacks but they had been unable to hold it without assistance from Moscow. Turkey regained it by the Treaty of Prut in 1711. In 1736 it was again taken by Russia. The effect of this victory upon Russia and all of Europe can not be overestimated. At one stroke, Peter made of Russia a power, which had to be considered in European politics. At home he took care to ascribe his success to his European reforms and gained thereby much support for his policy of Europeanization. Many people felt that if the reforms already instituted could produce such signal foreign success, there were no limits to which further reforms might not carry the country. He

After the success at Azov, Peter resolved to attempt first a peaceful penetration of the West before following out his original plans against Sweden, hoped to bring about an alliance of all Christian European nations, with Russia as the leader, against the Turks and Mohammedans in general. If this plan succeeded, he believed that he would be able to treat with Sweden concerning the territory he desired without the necessity of going to war. He, therefore, began to organize a suite which should accompany him on a tour through the principal countries of Europe, for the purpose of observing and studying all phases of the life of the people. This suite was known as "The Great Ambassadors of the Tsar" and consisted of 270 persons selected with the greatest care by Peter, himself. While their mission was unofficial, and Peter travelled incognito as Peter Michailov, its prime purpose and his great ambition was to secure for Russia a place of dominance among the nations.

The "ambassadors" left Russia in March 1697 and visited many of the chief cities of Europe, such as Riga, Konigsberg, Amsterdam and London, gaining much valuable information thereby, but Peter's plan for a great European alliance did not materialize. The time was not propitious. Europe was torn by the conflicts of the Hapsburgs and the Bourbons, and although there had been general satisfaction over the fall of Azov, the great powers had no intention of looking to Russia for future leadership. Realizing that he could not secure the alliance, Peter gave up his efforts and returned to Moscow in August 1698. 

In this connection it is important to note that the group of carefully selected young men who accompanied Peter abroad and who were expected thereafter, to play a leading role in the Europeanization of Russia, also imbibed the advanced political theories current in Britain and other parts of Western Europe. No sooner was Peter dead than they demanded a constitutional government for Russia. They may, in fact, be regarded as the progenitors of the Decembrists of 1825 and of subsequent agitators for political reform.

SPAT 34 

A.

Immediately after his return Peter began preparations for a campaign against the Swedes. In order that he might not be harassed by attacks from other sources, he sought and secured treaties with King Augustus II of Poland and King Christian of Denmark. By the summer of 1700, he had also negotiated a treaty of peace with Turkey. 

Thus assured, he began his campaign against Sweden. He met with constant reverses at first. The battle of Narva ended in a disastrous defeat for his forces and it required four years of dogged persistence and stubborn fighting on his part before he was able to conquer the desired Swedish territory of Ingria. Finally he became master of the site, which he had long had in mind as a desirable "window to Europe, and in May, 1703, he was at last able to found his new capital of St. Petersburg. 

The campaign and the building of the city caused so much suffering and cost so many lives that discontent and rebellion became general. Peter was nevertheless able to put down all revolts effectively. Then came the overwhelming defeat of the Swedes in the great battle of Poltava, in 1709. Peter was once more the national idol and popular discontent was stilled. 11 Although Sweden's power was broken, her king, Charles XII (1682-1718), escaped to Turkey. There he had little difficulty in inciting the Turks, never very friendly to Russia, to declare war against her late in 1710. The struggle which followed was nothing but a series of defeats for the Russian forces. The situation seemed so hopeless that Peter was ready to sue for peace when overtures for a cessation of hostilities came from Turkey. A treaty was concluded in 1711, by the terms of which Russia lost Azov, but was not compelled to pay any more severely for her sorry showing thoughout the war. This fortunate turn of affairs for Russia has been crodited to efforts put forth by Peter's second wife, Catherine.

In 1716, five years after the Russo-Turkish War (1710-1711), Peter the Great made a second European tour. His first visit, nineteen years earlier in 1697, had been chiefly for the purpose of collecting information and establishing diplomatic relations. Upon this occasion, however, he was concerned mainly with scientific matters.

At a very early age he had evinced a deep interest in science, and during his reign he had lent encouragement and assistance to scientific enterprise. Thus, immediately upon his accession to the throne, he had taken cognizance of the efforts of his energetic Cossacks, who were blazing new trails in Siberia, and had sent to their aid certain Swedish prisoners of war, who gave them intelligent guidance and taught them "to build sea-going vessels, to use nautical instruments and to construct modern maps."

Peter was, likewise, instrumental in bringing about the discovery and exploration of Kamchatka and the Kurile Islands, which took place between 1700 and 1715.

When reports of these accomplishments reached the scientific world, scholars of western Europe began to pay homage to Peter and to the achievements of Russian men of science. Oxford University conferred an honorary doctor's degree upon Peter I, and the Paris academy made him one of its members. Therefore when he came among 

European scientists in 1716-17, he was cordially received and urged to make explorations for the purpose of determining once and for all whether Asia and America were united.

Upon his return to Russia in 1718, he, therefore, ordered two of his officers, Fedor Luzhin and Ivan Evreinov, "to go to Kamchatka and farther..., and determine whether Asia and America are united; and go not only north and south but east and west, and put on a chart all that you see."

Although this expedition (1719-1722) failed to establish the fact in question, it was not entirely without results. Peter's interest did not flag and on December 

23, 1724, shortly before his death, he drew up orders for further explorations, thereby paving the way for the famous Bering expeditions (1725-1730; 1733-1742), the 

Gvozdev expedition (1731-1733), and the Baranov explorations (1790-1818).* "When Bering's second expedition came to an end in 1749," says Golder (Bering's Voyages, p. 5), "Bering Strait had been discovered, the Arctic coast of Asia from the White Sea to the Kolyma River had been charted, and the North Pacific coast of America from Cape Addington to Bering Island had been placed on the map. This was 

Russia's share in the work of discovery and exploration, and a very important contribution to geographical knowledge it was." 

Thus it was that, stimulated by Peter's energy and encouragement, explorations were made which opened a window to America and eventually solved the problem as to "whether Asia and America are united." While Russia's territorial expansion elsewhere was distinctly imperialistic, the explorations in America were motivated chiefly by scientific interest, albeit "for the benefit and glory of Russia" (Russian Senate). 

Despite the loss of prestige, which the clash with Turkey had cost him, Peter did not relinquish his efforts to force from the Swedes the entire extent of territory which he desired. In 1721 his obstinacy finally triumphed, and Sweden ceded the 

Baltic states to Russia, thus bringing to an end several centuries of conflict. In recognition of his conquest, the Senate bestowed upon him the titles, "Father of his 

Country," "Emperor," and "The Great" (Pater Patriae, Imperator Maximum). In order to strengthen his hold upon the Baltic territories, he sought to win foreign support *The question whether Asia and America were united was not answered until the coming of another Russian, Baron Ferdinand Wrangel, who, during the winters of 1821 and 1823, walked along the Arctic coast from the Kolyma to Kolyuchin Island.

American coast on September 1, 1732, but thought, however, that he had discovered an "island."

Company for nearly twenty-eight years and first governor of Russian America, rendered valuable service in the North Pacific. Under his direction the Russian possessions in the New World attained their widest extent. In 1796 he established a colony on 

Bering Strait. In 1799 he took possession of the largest of the Sitka Islands (now 

Baranov Islands), began trade with the natives, and subsequently extended his operations to Canton, the Hawaiian Islands, Boston, New York and other distant regions.

His daughter, Anne, was married to the Duke of Holstein; his niece, Catherine, to the Duke of Mecklenburg while another niece became the wife of the Duke of Courland. These marriages for the sake of diplomatic relations were productive later of much misfortune to Russia. 

Peter's success in the Baltic region marked the culmination and well nigh the end of his career. Despite his robust constitution, he was not destined to reach. extreme old age. Early in November: 1724, he happened to see a boat in distress. 

Always courageous, he plunged into the icy water to go to its assistance. He was seized with an illness as a result of this exposure from which he never recovered. died on February 5, 1725, at the age of 53. 2. 

Reign of Empress Catherine I (1725-1727) 

Another He

Peter's heir apparent, his son Alexei by his first wife, Eudokia Lopukhina, whom he put away in 1698, had been murdered by Peter's own orders in 1718. son, Alexander, had died at an early age. Although the crown might naturally have passed to his grandson, Peter named his second wife, Catherine, as his successor. 

Her accession, which was an unheard-of innovation, far greater than the regency of Sophia, aroused the bitterest opposition, and many adherents of the Orthodox Church submitted to torture rather than take an oath of allegiance to her. They objected to her not only because she was a woman and not of royal descent, but also because she had been a captive and though legally the second wife of Peter, her repute was scarcely more than that of his mistress.* Nevertheless, despite all opposition she was crowned empress in May, 1724, and succeeded him to the throne on February 19, 1725. Her short reign was marked by no significant event, and was in reality merely a sequel to the reign of Peter. Shortly before her death (May 17), she named as her successor Peter's grandson, Peter, son of the murdered Alexei by his first wife, Sophia Charlotte of Wolfenfuettel. Next in the line of succession she named her daughters, Anna of Holstein and Elizabeth. 

3. Reign of Emperor Peter II (1727-1730). 

Peter II was twelve years old at the time of his accession and lived only three years longer, dying of smallpox on the eve of his marriage. Considering his extreme youth and short reign, we would not look for any startling changes or spectacular events during this period. As a matter of fact, however, his reign was marked by certain significant occurrences, which seemed to give confirmation to the fear of Peter I that the reactionary blood of Alexei would utterly destroy everything that he (Peter) had accomplished with so much difficulty. In any event, the Church and the boyars again became active participants in public affairs and the Imperial Court was transferred from St. Petersburg to Moscow, although the former city still remained the capital. *She was of Swedish birth. Her parents died when she was very young and a preacher's family, Gluck by name, took her into their home as a servant. In 1701 she married a soldier in the Swedish army, but only two days after the marriage, she was taken prisoner by Russian soldiers and separated forever from her husband. Because of her beauty, Field Marshall Boris Petrovitch Sheremetiev made her his mistress. Later General Menshikov took her into his house. Here Peter I saw her, and fell in love with her, made her his mistress, and three years later, his wife. She was only twenty years old at the time of her marriage to Peter.
