as a minority group suffered especially. The one ruler to throw off the influence of clerical instruction and select as his assistants men whom he needed, without regard for rank or station, was Peter the Great. Under him a Polish Jew, Shaffirov, even became Vice-Chancellor, and accompanied the Emperor on his famous travels. 

As has been mentioned before, Catherine II did not indulge in religious persecutions and even offered an asylum to the persecuted of other lands; nevertheless, she placed restrictions upon the Jews. She was constrained to do this both by the clergy and by certain business people, who were finding it increasingly difficult to compete successfully with the Jews. Catherine, therefore, in 1791, established a Pale of Settlement, or segregated district, in which the Jews were compelled to live. In these districts, or Ghettos, they came in contact with few 

Russians other than uneducated peasants, together with a few unfriendly and prejudiced members of the Clergy and public officials. These latter oppressed and bled the Jews wherever the occasion offered. Educational opportunities being as limited as they were, Jews had not the least chance of entering Russian schools. 

Consequently they remained entirely ignorant of the Russian language and Russian life. They lived to themselves and used the Talmud as their sole spiritual and cultural guide. It is not surprising, therefore, that by reason of the association with the most ignorant and oppressive among the Gentiles, they began to regard all of them with detestation and distrust. On the other hand, any members of the upper classes, who occasionally came in contact with the Jews, considered them uncultured and often lacking in cleanliness. Thus there existed mutual misunderstanding and hostility. Despite this, however, the Jews were very loyal to the emperors, and built up industry greatly in their districts. 

This then, was the situation at the accession of Nicholas I, who had his own ideas concerning the handling of the Jewish problem in his territories. Under his personal supervision, almost 600 laws were promulgated concerning the Jews, most of which were in line with his general policy of militarizing and amalgamating the diverse elements of his empire. In 1827 for the first time, Jews were required to render active service in the army. Heretofore it was generally accepted that if conscripted, they could pay a specified sum for exemption. Nicholas, however, looked upon this service as an effective method of solving the Jewish problem. 

Recruits were taken from the ages of twelve to twenty-five for a compulsory military service of twenty-five years. The Emperor foresaw that during this long period, far from their homes, they would not only become superlatively well-trained soldiers, but they would also forget their early religious teaching, and accept the Orthodox faith. This amounted to compulsory conversion.

In order to encourage further the acceptance of Orthodox faith, the government resorted to various expedients, not the least of which was the offer of all the rights and privileges of Christians to baptized Jews. Two measures in their favor also met with response and approbation from some of the more far-sighted among them. One opened Russian schools to the Jews, the other made it possible for them to enter into agricultural pursuits. However, because in the past they had frequently been the victims of tricks and deception, the majority of them looked with suspicion upon any overtures of good-will on the part of the government, sens ing therein a subtle attack which would rob them of their Judaism. It is unfortunate in the extreme that there was not a more general response among them to the agricultural program. Had they left the congested cities and settled upon the land they would have escaped much persecution, and the country would not have been disgraced by pograms. There were, to be sure, many Jewish

100

colonies established in the provinces of Ekaterinoslav (now Dniepropetrovsk) and Kherson, while by the time of the Revolution of 1917, some 100,000 Jews were engaged in tilling the soil. These Jewish farmers had not suffered nearly so much persecution during the years as had their co-religionists in the cities. 

It will be remembered that during the reign of Alexei there were two movements sponsoring opposing theories concerning the best manner in which Russia could fulfill her destiny. Under the impetus of the Decembrist uprising, these movements became well-organized groups, possessed of well-defined ideology. At this time they came to be known as Slavophiles and the Westernizers. The leaders of the former, in theory at least, were A. S. Khamiakov, the Kireevski brothers, I. T. 

Samarin, and the Aksakov brothers. They held that if Russia would assert herself and follow her own natural lines of development, she would attain results beneficial to herself and to all humanity as well. Never would she realize her true destiny by imitation or adaptation of Western ideas, for every nation as well as every individual was possessed of a unique personality, and only by full development of this true personality could success be attained. Further they believed that in the 

Orthodox faith, Russia had preserved the purest form of Christianity, far superior to anything which the West had to offer. Thus when Peter the Great had weakened the influence of the Orthodox faith and had forcibly introduced Western innovations, he had torn the land asunder, wresting from it its true tradition, and doing incomparable harm thereby. The only salvation lay in a return to former beliefs, ideals and modes of life. It occasions no surprise that Nicholas I was himself a 

Slavophile, since for him Orthodoxy and autocracy were the inviolable principles of government.

In contrast to all of this, the Westernizers whose leaders were V. G. 

Belinsky, T. N. Granovsky, and A. I. Herzen, I. S. Turgenev, etc., advocated fol lowing the course of European progress. They opposed the reactionary clergy and favored liberalism or socialism in politics. For them a return to the old order of the days preceding Peter I was equivalent to a return to the dark ages. They ap- proved of Peter's reforms and held that he had rendered an inestimable service by his program of Europeanization. Both Slavophiles and Westernizers consulted German philosophy as expressed by Schelling and Hegel in an effort to find support for their theories.

The most important internal improvements of the reign of Nicholas I were in the field of transportation and communications. In 1838 a private company undertook the construction of Russia's first railroad, which was completed between St. 

Petersburg and Tsarskoye-Selo. By 1842 another railroad was begun between St. 

Petersburg and Moscow, this time at state expense. The completion of this road took eight years and cost 100,000,000 rubles, even with the employment of slave labor. By 1875 there had been constructed in Russia 17,000 versts of railroad, or nearly 13,000 miles. In 1851 St. Petersburg and Moscow were also connected by telegraph.

B. Foreign Affairs: 

Nicholas I did not favor revolution abroad any more than at home, and was ever ready to throw the weight of his influence against it. For example, when the 

Bourbons in France were overthrown by the July revolution of 1830, Nicholas prepared to intervene in their favor and was only prevented from so doing by the

hot sterol to Motala 

TOL foeb rad 1LINGE 009 Lin perse

A mole

outbreak of a revolution in Poland.* Also in 1848-49, when all of Europe was in the grip of revolt, Nicholas again stood forth as a reactionist. In response to the request of Emperor Francis Joseph of Austria in 1849, he sent 100,000 men, who assisted in crushing the Hungarian uprising. This service earned for Nicholas the title of the "policeman of Europe." Wars in the Near East broke out shortly after the accession of Nicholas. 

The first of these was with Persia. It was ended by the treaty of Turkmanchay in 

1828, by which Russia gained the territories on the left bank of the Arax, together with Erivan. In addition Persia paid a heavy indemnity and gave up her claim upon the Caucasus. Almost immediately, however, the Muridist revolution, a religious movement, broke out among the Moslem moun ta ineers in the Caucasus. Under the leadership of able imams and sheikhs, the best known of whom were Kazi-Mullah, who was killed in 1831 and Shanyl, who was captured by the Russians in 1859, the revolutionists maintained a long and stubborn resistance and were not fully subdued until the reign of Alexander II. The conquest of the Caucasus took in all 137 years from 1722 to 1859. In 1722 Peter the Great invaded Dagestan and established garrisons at Tarki and Baku. During the years 1806 to 1813 under Alexander I, Russia acquired many provinces through treaty. 

Scarcely had peace been concluded with Persia, when Russia was again involved in war, this time with Turkey. This conflict graw out of the Greek struggle for independence. For a long time in conformity with the sentiment of the "Holy 

Alliance," Europe rendered no assistance to the Greek rebels. However when Turkish atrocities became too appalling, Russia, England and France protested to the Sultan, and sought to procure peace between the combatants by diplomatic means. Failing in this, they took drastic action and sent an allied fleet against Turkey which defeated the Turkish Squadron at Navarino on November 2, 1827, In 1828 Russia and

Turkey engaged in combat which came to an end through the peace of Adrianople in 1829. The terms of the treaty were most favorable to Russia, inasmuch as she gained possession of the mouth of the Danube and the coast of the Black Sea to Poti. 

Turkey also acceded to Russia's demand for a protectorate over Orthodox people s living in Turkish territories and besides granted commercial privileges to Russian subjects in the same regions. In addition to her concessions to Russia, Turkey agreed to recognize the independence of the Greeks (1830) and to open the Bosporus and Dardanelles to friendly nations. Moreover certain dependencies of Turkey, namely Moldavia, Wallachia, Serbia and the principalities of the Danube, were permitted to establish their own governments under Turkish suzerainty. 

Nicholas might have required far more by the treaty of Adrianople, but he preferred to moderate his demands and pursue a policy of expansion by peaceful penetration in conformity with the tactics of Catherine II in the Middle and Far East and those of Paul in the Near East. Therefore when the Pasha of Egypt began hostilities against the Sultan in 1833, he sent a force to Asia Minor to protect the Bosporus. This resulted in the treaty of Unkiar-Skelessi, 1833 (August 8), by which the Emperor's diplomacy scored a great victory, for by its terms the *Toward the close of the year 1830, the Poles, encouraged by the French and Belgian revolutions, staged a revolt of their own. They took possession of Warsaw, forcing the troops of Grand Duke Constantine to evacuate the city. They then demanded their independence, together with the surrender of Lithuania and Western Russia. The Emperor's answer was to send a strong force against Poland under General Diebitsche The revolt was crushed and Poland was made part of the empire and divided into provinces like the rest of Russia.

Sultan closed the Bosporus and the Dardanelles to the war-ships of all foreign powers except Russia. This treaty, however, won for Nicholas the enmity of France and England, which was further intensified by the agreement which he made the same year with Prussia and Austria. 

The foreign policy of Nicholas I during the eighteen-forties was characterized by two fixed ideas: (1) a firm determination to stop the spread of democracy and liberalism in Europe; (2) the acquisition of the Dardanelles and Constantinople. 

In 1844, in pursuit of the second objective, he visited England, hoping to obtain some assurance of English neutrality in the event of another Russian war against 

Turkey, which he labelled the "Sick Man of Europe."

In reality, Nicholas achieved neither of his objectives. Not only did he fail to call a halt to democratic and liberal trends in Europe, but that continent became a hotbed of revolution in 1848. By using Russian troops for the suppression of revolt abroad, Nicholas brought Russia into disrepute among Western liberals for years to come as a reactionary and barbaric country. In the second place, Nicholas' bid for Constantinople and the Dardanelles led directly to the Crimean 

War (1853-56). 

The immediate cause of the Crimean War, however, grew out of the efforts of Napoleon III of France to strengthen French influence and prestige in the Near East, particularly in the Holy Land. He demanded and obtained from Turkey concessions for Roman Catholics living in the Holy Land. He obtained for them also possession of the keys of the Church of Bethlehem, which by the treaty of Kuchuk-Kainard ji (1774) had been assigned to the keeping of the Orthodox Church. Nicholas demanded that the treaty be respected and Orthodox rights restored. When Turkey refused to do this, he invaded Moldavia and Wallachia. This was followed by a declaration of war by Turkey in the autumn of 1853. 

Russia's first move was to send the Black Sea Squadron against the Turkish fleet, which resulted in the destruction of the latter at Sinope. On January 27, 1854, England and France declared war on Russia. They dispatched their fleets to the Black Sea, and were soon joined by reinforcements from Sardinia. Austria and 

Prussia, formerly friendly to Russia, showed a decidedly hostile front, although they remained neutral. Therefore Russia faced an enemy alignment of the chief powers of Western Europe. In the autumn of 1854, the allied forces entered Crimea near Eupatoria and laid siege to Sevastopol. The city surrendered after a siege which lacked only fifteen days of lasting a year. Nicholas did not live to see its downfall, for he died on March 2, 1855. It was rumored that he committed suicide by taking poison because he was unable to outlive the defeat of all his plans and the collapse of all his hopes. Peace was concluded in 1856 by his son, Alexander II, under the terms of the Treaty of Paris. 

The Treaty of Paris destroyed at one stroke the results of two centuries of effort on the part of Russian rulers. While Sevastopol remained in her possession, Russia lost control of the Black Sea. She was also compelled to relinquish her exclusive protectorate over Orthodox Christians in the near East, all the Great Powers sharing the responsibility for all Christian residents in Turkish lands. addition the Bosporus and the Dardanelles were closed to all armed fleets of any nationality whatever. In Thus the military prestige which Russia had built up and the influence which she had been able to exert throughout Europe were entirely swept away, The Treaty of Paris sounded the beginning of the end of Russian expansion in Europe.

FROM LIBERATOR TO THE LAST EMPEROR (Alexander II to Nicholas II, 1855-1894) 1. Reign of Emperor Alexander II (1855-1881). 

Although Alexander became emperor by the death of his father on March 2, 1855, the continuation of the war and the events following its conclusion delayed his coronation until the next year. Because of the noteworthy and beneficial reforms which he introduced into his distracted country, he was very much praised by subsequent generations, who bestowed upon him such titles as the White Tsar, the 

Liberator, the Great Reformer, the Abraham Lincoln of Russia. At the beginning of his reforms the Westernizers rejoiced in his liberalism, going even so far as to attribute it to his English descent on his mother's side. 

Born in St. Petersburg on April 29, 1818, Alexander became emperor at the age of thirty-six. Whereas his father, Nicholas I, had received no special training for the responsibilities of an emperor, inasmuch as his accession had seemed remote, all of Alexander's training was directed with a view to preparing him adequately for his high office. His education had been most liberal and his experience in statecraft most adequate by the time he was called upon to assume the reins of government. One of his tutors was the poet Zhukovsky, who translated the Iliad and the Odyssey into Russian and whose nobility of character was generally recognized. At the age of twenty-three, he married Maria Alexandrovna, Princess of 

Hesse-Darmstadt. Thoy had eight children, six sons and two daughters.

A. 

Internal Reforms: 

It is no secret that shortly before his death, Nicholas I, with unusual honesty and recognition of fact, admitted to his son that he had made a mistake in his Slavophile and autocratic policies. The crumbling of the Russian defense before a Western European attack had made it clear that a government of such extreme autocracy as that which Nicholas I had instituted, was suited, if at all, only to Asiatic peoples. This was generally admitted even by enthusiastic Slavophiles. 

Thus Aksakov affirmed that "Sevastopol fell that God might reveal all the rottenness of the system of government." Yet another ardent Slavophile, Samarin, said: "We have fallen, not before the forces of the Western Alliance, but as a result of our own internal weakness." Nicholas advised Alexander, therefore, to follow more liberal methods upon his accession. Though publicly, Alexander expressed diplomatic vindication of his father's policy and attempted to smooth over the humiliating terms of the Treaty of Paris, he lost no time in instituting a different policy of his own, devised to repair the ravages of past mistakes. The day of his coronation was marked by the issuance of a manifesto of 38 articles. Among its provisions was an amnesty for surviving Decembrists of the 1825 uprising and for Polish conspirators of the 1831 revolution. Further, children who had been forcibly inducted into the army and navy were to be returned to their families and permitted to make their own choice of a profession. 62

QUA With these beginnings, Alexander II undertook at once the major problems which confronted him, and in the course of time instituted reforms in respect to the following: Serfdom, Zemstvos, Court Procedure, and Universal Military Service. 

We shall now proceed to consider his reforms in order, beginning with the one which he considered of major importance. At the time of Alexander's accession, the population of Russia was about 70,000,000. Of this number 47,000,000 were living in a more or less arduous condition of serfdom. Some of them, to be sure, were free in everything but name. 

These were the 20,000,000 crown peasants, who had became dependents of the state by the process of redemption previously mentioned. In addition to these, there were 

4,7000,000 on appanages, or Udyely. These peasants had their own communities, wherein they exercised a degree of self government and managed their private affairs in accordance with their own traditions. The problem which they presented to the state was comparatively easy to handle, consisting as it did of little more than official confirmation of the freedom which they already enjoyed. By the middle of 

1858, an edict of the government made them free in law as well as in fact. 

The cancerous spot in the system of serfdom, which required the most diplomatic treatment to effect its cure, was to be found in private ownership of serfs; actually 22,400,000 peasants were virtually slaves of individual landowners. The bondage of the 1,400,000 of these who served as domestic servants (Dvorovie or Kholopy) was especially strict; Alexander II directed his efforts, therefore, toward the abolition of this type of servitude. 

He was obliged to move with caution and circumspection, for while a large majority of the people was in favor of freeing the serfs, there was an opposing faction, composed of rich landowners and reactionary public officials, who were both influential and powerful. It was necessary to secure their support before any drastic reform could be instituted with any hope of success. The Emperor, therefore, sought to win the cooperation of these classes by an appeal to practical reasoning. 

In an address delivered before their leaders in Moscow, he pointed out succinctly that it was much better for them to handle the problem while it was still under their control, than to wait until their hands were forced by uprisings, which would result in destruction of property and loss of life. "Better that the reform should come from above than wait until serfdom is abolished from below." This made a great impression, for the owners of the serfs, could not fail to recognize the soundness of his reasoning. Practical considerations for the handling of the matter necessitated long discussions, deliberations and adjustments, but finally on March 3, 1861, the sixth anniversary of his accession, the Emperor issued the manifesto abolishing serfdom from Russia. If the reign of Alexander had been distinguished solely by the issuance of this manifesto it would merit a place of importance in Russian annals, and he himself would be entitled to the gratitude accorded him by his subjects. Despite the emancipation, however, the peasant problem was far from solved, for there remained the question of the ownership and use of the land. The peasants continued to live on the land and till the soil, but as freemen, they were obliged to pay for these privileges either in money, produce, or service, for the land remained the property of the fomer owner. If the latter wished to sell to the peasants, he might do so; and government assistance was provided in the form of long term loans to facilitate the transaction. Indeed by the death of Alexander in 1881, more than 80 percent of the land owners had sold out to the peasants. Here, however, we reach a most important phase of the matter, which, if properly understood, explains much of Russia's later economic and social difficulties. The land, thus

beropteroo ci 68

acquired by the peasants, did not belong to them individually but was held in colmunal ownership by an entire group. While each member thereof shared in the responsibility of paying off the purchase price of the land, none of it became a private possession. It is evident that such an arrangement closely resembled a 

Soviet Kolkhoz, or Collective Farm of the nineteen-twenties and in fact acted as the best possible training for economic developments which were to came in succeeding years. It should be recognized that the problem of land ownership was not efficiently handled and that the failure to do so without doubt precipitated the revolutions of 1905 and 1917. What could have been done to produce more general satisfaction and thereby avoid the aforementioned social catastrophies? It has been most ably demonstrated that there were two possible solutions, either of which would in all probability have prevented later misfortunes.* The government might have elected to give the peasants merely their personal freedom and assisted individuals, rather than groups. or communes, to purchase the land from the original owners. As a second mode of procedure, the government might have taken over the land with suitable compensation to its former owners and then parceled it out among the peasants, again on an individual basis. In either case, by the year 1917, there would have been in 

Russia millions of farmers instead of millions of peasants, which would have constituted a conservative, agricultural middle class. It was for the purpose of developing such a class, that Premier Stolypin instituted his reforms of 1906-1911, but it was then too late. There is no doubt that the success of Russia's social revolution was due to the lack of a middle class. 

The stability of any country is in a large measure dependent upon the middle class, and in proportion as this class is strong and well established, the danger of a social revolution along communistic lines is lessened. The capitalist, with extensive schemes and high ambitions, may risk his entire capital in a speculative venture. He may lose everything, but on the other hand, his gains may exceed all dreams of avarice. He enjoys playing for a great stake. The laborer, especially in Europe, has very little to lose while he may gain much by a change in the social order. Hence a revolution becomes his speculative venture. A member of the middle class, however, is in an entirely different position from either of these representatives of two social extremes. He possesses a little property or a small capital, which has cost him years of toil and self denial to accumulate. He has become accustomed to some modest comforts. A revolution would rob him of all of this and plunge him into an abyss of destitution and privation. He has nothing to gain, and everything to lose, hence he is conservative in the extreme. Any government which takes cognizance of its middle class and furthers its interests has little to fear in respect to revolution and communism. This is the case in the 

United States where the middle class is very large and has a somewhat high standard of living. The chief reason why other European countries did not go communistic after World War I as did Russia is to be found in their possession of a relatively large middle class. This explains also why the Soviet government in pursuance of its communistic ends was so ruthless in its treatment of the Kulak farmers, a product of the Stolypin reforms, 1906-1911. In passing, "If Russia had had a middle *In this matter the author concurs entirely with George Vernadsky.

class of any size," says Maurice Hindus, "the Bolshevik Revolution might never have become an active fact, or, if it had, it surely would have failed. With the abolition of serfdom, a change in rural government soon became necessary. Previously the nobility of a province had governed in behalf of all freemen residing therein. In 1864 a form of representative government, known as the Zemstvo, was introduced into 34 provinces. Under this order, all property owners were allowed to participate in local affairs, although the nobility had precedence in all respects. By representative vote an "Executive Committee" or "Zemstvo Committee" was elected for a term of three years. This body took charge of the local affairs of a province. By 1870 town government was managed by a similar system. The Zemstvos could levy taxes and had the supervision of roads, public health and schools. The central government, which had a veto in all matters, was careful to restrict the Zemstvo to local affairs, and often interfered even in this field. This was especially true in regard to the schools, which, as directed by the Zemstvos, were usually more liberal than the government desired. 

Hence there was considerable governmental supervision of the teaching body. Yet often, in spite of the interference of the government, the Zemstvo schools produced noteworthy results in their efforts to banish ignorance from the rural districts. 

Both the provincial Zemstvo and the town government were responsible for much progress along material and cultural lines previous to the Revolution of 1917. If this had not been the case, the Revolution would have been marked by even more horrors and bloodshed. In 1914 the expenditure of the Zemstvos of 43 provinces was in the neighborhood of 400,000,000 rubles. Of this, the sum 106,000,000 rubles was devoted to public education. There were 50,000 Zemstvo schools employing 80,000 teachers and furnishing instruction to 3,000,000 children. All of this came to an end in 1917. 

Of equal importance to the reforms in local government were the reforms in the judiciary. The most outstanding of these were an alteration and improvement in court procedure, the introduction of trial by jury, and the organization of lawyers into a formal bar. Thus strengthened, the courts operated with a considerable degree of efficiency.

Another important reform of the reign of Alexander was the introduction of universal military service. However, it was by no means so rigid and autocratic as it had been under Nicholas I. Thus the long term of service was shortened to six years and family ties and responsibilities were respected An only son, an only grandson, or a son, who was the sole support of a family registered in what was known as the reserve of the Second Category (Vtoroye Opolchenie). This reserve previous to World War I was never called into active service. Likewise recruits possessing a secondary education received consideration in respect to term of service, promotion and certain privileges. While the officers of the Guard were drawn exclusively from the aristocracy the rest of the army was the most democratic institution in all Russia. Here the diverse races and creeds of the great expanse of the empire met on a footing of equality, and began to develop a better understanding of each other. *The Great Offensive, N.Y., 1933, p. 357. See also pp. 357-368. 

Chamberlin, W. N., Russia's Iron Age, 1934, pp. 228-229. See .

Ji neven 7009. 1017

Much of the harsh, restrictive legislation of Nicholas I was repealed and a more liberal, humanitarian policy adopted. In the army and the imperial tribunals, corporal punishment was legally abolished, though sometimes resorted to, nevertheless. In the peasant tribunals it still remained in force. Censorship of the press relaxed its vigilance and soon there sprang up in St. Petersburg and 

Moscow, periodicals which had a brisk circulation. By 1860 full civil rights were granted to foreign residents in confomity with the practice followed by other European countries. This did much to break down the barriers, which the policy of Nicholas had built up between Russia and her neighbors. Restrictions were removed from the universities. The number of students was no longer limited to 300; the cost of tuition was lowered, and scholarships were created for the encouragement of talent and ability. Most noteworthy along educational lines was the admission of Jews to the gymnasiums and universities. Alexander II extended many other privileges to the Jews. On March 16, 1857, and November 27, 1861, he enacted laws which permitted Jewish scholars, university graduates, wholesale merchants and manufacturers to reside outside of the Pale of Settlement. In 1865 this privilege, with certain restrictions, was also granted to artisans. The Emperor would have been glad to abolish the Pales entirely, but he dared not oppose the bureaucracy. The Jews were fully cognizant of his good will, and his policy did more to Russianize them than all previous resorts to force and compulsion. Indeed if the Jews had continued to enjoy these privileges and had been allowed to associate with other citizens on a basis of freedom and equality, very few would have remained by 1917, for by that time they would have been completely absorbed by the Russian people. The pogroms, following the reign of Alexander II once more set them apart and made them keenly aware of their 

Judaism. 

Poland,

In pursuance of his policy of liberalism, Alexander II tried to calm the seething unrest in Poland by granting the country self government. He appointed 

Grand Duke Constantine Nikolayevitch as viceroy, because of the latter's Polish sympathies and liberalism and established complete local autonomy under him. however, so resented Russian control, that the overtures of the Emperor were answered by an outbreak of rebellion. Russian garrisons in Poland and Lithuania were attacked, and the revolt began to assume formidable proportions. The seriousness of the situation was further intensified by the interference of Austria, France and England. The reactionists now took the opportunity to charge that the reforms had been carried to extremes, and were the cause of all sorts of confusion and disaster. The Emperor yielded to their insistence, suppressed the revolt without mercy and then took away local autonomy. In order to gain some support in the rebellious territory, he granted privileges to the peasants at the expense of the nobles.

B. Foreign Policy: 

During the first part of his reign, Alexander II devoted all his efforts to securing a revision of the Treaty of Paris. Especially did he desire the removal of the restriction keeping the Russian fleet from the Black Sea. 

He perseveringly sought this revision, inasmuch as he had by no means relinquished the plans of his predecessors for territorial expansion. The project as outlined by 

Peter the Great in 1717, aimed at completing the conquest of the Caucasus and pushing territorial expansion in the Middle East (Central Asia) and the Far East. 

Nicholas I had devoted much thought and study to the project, and had worked out a plan of action with the details of which Alexander II was thoroughly conversant. 

He now only awaited an opportunity to throw off the shackles of the Treaty of Paris,

which opportunity was afforded him by the Franco-Prussian War in 1870. In return for the "benevolent neutrality" of Russia during the Austro-Prussian War (1866) and the Franco-Prussian War (1870-71), Prussia refused to join England in protest against the unilateral denunciation by Russia of the Black Sea clause of the Treaty of Paris. As a result, the offensive clause was repealed by the powers signatory to the Paris Treaty at a convention in London in 1871. Alexander then proceeded with all speed to push the conquest of the Caucasus, with such success that by the end of his reign, Kokand, Bokhara, Khiva, together with such of their dependencies as Kirghiz, Turkman and Uzbeks had acknowledged Russian supremacy. Before the Crimean War, official Russian opinion was divided as to the wisdom of expansion in the Far East. Such expansion as took place was mainly the product of individual initiative. As a result of the Russian defeat in Europe, however, the monarchy lent official sanction and support to prominent and colorful Russian expansionists like Count Nikolai N. Muraviev. In consequence, Russia's territorial gains in the Far East were considerable. By the Treaty of Aygun of 

1858 the Amur region, which had been lost to China in 1689 by the Treaty of Nerchinsk, was regained. Two years later (1860) the Ussuri Territory likewise came under Russian control. In 1875, Russia gave the Kurile Islands to Japan in exchange for the southern half of the Island of Sakhalin. The Great Powers, especially England, watched this growing expansion with increasing uneasiness. 

Russia's success in Turkestan was particularly objectionable to England, in view of her possessions in India. Finally Alexander found it expedient to agree to the establishment of a neutral zone between Russian and English possessions in the 

Middle East.

Realizing the hostility of the European powers, Russia from time to time had sought a rapprochement with the United States. The dislike which both countries felt for England and a similarity in their social problems of serfdom and slavery made a mutual understanding not improbable. Nevertheless, the rapprochement did not progress beyond the lending of moral support upon a few occasions. For instance, the United States demonstrated its goodwill to Russia at the time of the Crimean War. In return for this, the Russian government came out openly in favor of the Union forces during the Civil War and sent a fleet in confirmation of its stand. A few years later, in 1867, the Tsarist government, in order to avoid the possibility of a future clash with the Americans, sold Alaska to the United States for 15,000,000 rubles (about $7,200,000). However Alexander's policy of imperialism could win no approval in the United States and American diplomacy was, as yet, definitely committed to the policy of avoiding "foreign entanglements." Therefore 

Alexander gave up his efforts in this direction and in 1872 brought about an understanding with Germany and Austria.

The Russo-Turkish War (1877-78) was caused by Turkish oppression of Slavic peoples under her control. Since Great Britain did not intervene, Montenegro and 

Serbia made war on Turkey in 1876. The government of Serbia placed its army under the command of an able Russian general. Nevertheless, all that saved Serbia from a crushing defeat was Russia's declaration of war on Turkey on April 24, 1877, Rumania also entered the struggle against the Turks. By the following February, the 

Russian forces were at Constantinople. This stirred England to action, and a 

British fleet was sent to the Sea of Marmora. Within a month, however, Russia and 

Turkey were negotiating terms of peace. On March 3, 1878, by the treaty of San 

Stefano, signed in a small village of that name, Turkey made great concessions. 

From the Turkish Balkan possessions between the Danube and the Aegean Sea, the new principality of Bulgaria was to be formed to include the river Vardar and all of Macedonia. Turkey also recognized the independence of Serbia, Montenegro and

Rumania. For her share, Russia regained the mouth of the Danube, which she had lost in 1856, and in addition, Batum and Kars in Transcaucasia. In pressing the terms of this treaty, Alexander committed a diplomatic error. In taking so much for Russia, he aroused the envy of other European powers having interests in the Balkans, namely England and Austria. Since, in 1875, in response to the urgent personal appeal of Queen Victoria, Alexander had exerted pressure upon Germany not to renew its war against France, he had assumed that both England and France would allow him a free hand in the Balkans. This did not prove to be the case. 

A general European war seemed imminent. However Germany took the initiative through her chancellor, Bismark, and succeeded in bringing about a conference of the powers for the purpose of revising the Treaty of San Stefano. This Congress met in Berlin in 1878 and resulted in the complete defeat of Russian diplomacy, leaving the country without friends or allies. In the first place, the territory of Bulgaria was drastically reduced. Macedonia was restored to Turkey, and the remaining region was divided into two parts, namely, Bulgaria and Eastern Rumelia, both of which were reduced to dependencies of Turkey. Meanwhile Austria was permitted to occupy Bosnia and Herzegovina "temporarily." Bismark who had favored 

Austria much beyond England's expectations now entered into a secret alliance with Austria and Italy. This "Triple Alliance" was to function in case of war with Russia.

Naturally Bulgaria and all Balkan Slavic peoples were very much dissatisfied with the results of the Berlin conference. They felt that Russia had not been firm enough but had sacrificed their interests to save her face. In consequence they distrusted Tsarist Russia in the future. Thus Bulgaria joined the 

Central Powers against the Allies in the World War I and became a German satellite in World War II. 

In Both Nicholas I and Alexander II failed in their attempts to capture Constantinople. It was not until Turkey joined the Central Powers in World War I that the Tsarist government renewed its efforts to gain control of the Straits. the spring of 1915, by secret treaty, England and France agreed to Russian annexation of the Straits, including Constantinople, once victory had been achieved. 

Alexander faced great difficulties of adjustment within the country as well as outside. During his entire reign he made earnest efforts to improve the condition of all his people by instituting worth-while reforms. Nevertheless he had to endure criticism and opposition from both the liberals and the reactionists. 

The former accused him of procrastination in instituting reforms, which were after all only half-way measures. The reactionists raised the cry that he was undermining the very foundations of government by his concessions to the liberals. Neither party took into consideration the difficulties under which the Emperor labored. 

The reactionists failed to recognize that without his reforms the country might easily have been ravaged by revolts, of which hostile foreign powers would have taken advantage. The liberals, for their part, did not take into account that he had to proceed by inches and make concessions in order to put through his various reforms. 

After the failure of his foreign policy, he resolved nevertheless to go further with his liberal, internal reforms and grant a constitutional government to his people. In 1880, therefore, he organized an Executive Committee under the leadership of Count Loris-Melikov, which was instructed to draft a constitution. 

The constitutional document was submitted to him on March 13, 1881, and signed forthwith. On the very next day, before it could be made the subject of a

proclamation, the Emperor was assassinated by radical conspirators. His son and successor, Alexander III, rescinded the constitution and the ukaz of Alexander II was never published. 

2. Reign of Emperor Alexander III (1881-1894). 

The entire country was shocked by the assassination of Alexander II, and a general reaction against liberalism immediately set in. Following the failure of his foreign policy, an economic depression had descended upon the country by which the nobility was especially impoverished. Many had been forced to sell their estates and take up their residence in the towns. Here they agitated incessantly for the return to a more autocratic policy in government under a strong, reactionary ruler. Such a ruler Alexander III proved to be.

All of his governmental measures were directed toward the restoration of an absolute autocracy, and the suppression of everything which savored of liberalism and revolution. Privileges were curtailed, and former restrictions revived and intensified. In this favorable atmosphere, religious persecution began once more to make itself manifest, as if it had been merely biding its time in anticipation of this opportunity. Since in Russia, autocratic government has always been closely associated with the Church, it occasions no surprise that the Emperor's former tutor, Pobyedonostzev, Procurator of the Holy Synod, was the directing spirit of much of the reign of terror which broke out shortly after the accession of Alexander 

III. 

While all non-Orthodox peoples and dissenters in general suffered, it was the Jews who were especially singled out for bitter and relentless persecution. 

Alexander III came to the throne in March, and in a little more than a month a series of atrocities were committed against the Jewish population in about 170 places throughout Southern European Russia. The se demonstrations of fiendish cruelty were known as pogroms and had the official sanction of the government and the blessing of the Orthodox Church. They were devised to draw attention away from certain revolutionary movements, which were threatening tsardom and to give to the murderous rabble an opportunity to satiate their lust for blood. Also because some of the Jewish youth had joined with Gentile compatriots in a movement against autocracy, the government pointed to the pogroms as an indication that the Russian people "had given vent" to their fury against revolutionists and like enemies of the Emperor.

As already indicated, religious persecution was not confined to the Jews alone, Roman Catholics and Protestants also being subject to it. In their case, however, the persecution consisted more in a curtailment of liberties with the purpose of forcing religious conformity. Thus throughout Poland and even in Finland, many privileges granted by Alexander II were withdrawn and any degree of selfgovernment was destroyed. In the Baltic provinces an attempt was made to eradicate the German cultural influence.

The following order signed by Alexander III in 1881 gives an eloquent picture of the conditions which existed in Russia at this time. "A man may be perfectly loyal; he may never have given expression to a single thought calculated to create disrespect for the Emperor, or the Emperor's Government, and yet, if he comes accidentally to know that his sister, or his brother, or his friend belongs to a society which contemplates a change in the existing form of government, and if he does not go voluntarily to the chief of the gendarmerie and betray that brother, sister, or friend, the law is adequate to send him to Siberia for life."

life.

It naturally follows that government censorship permeated every phase of The press and the universities, which were reorganized in 1884, were stripped of every semblance of liberty. The police exercised a supervision and operated with a license unheard of even in the days of Nicholas I. Political prisoners were regarded as having lost all rights, and were subjected to shocking brutality. A greater contrast to the liberalism of Alexander II could not well be imagined than that presented by the absolutism of Alexander III. In 1887 a plot to assassinate 

Alexander III was uncovered by the police. The leaders of the conspiracy were executed. Among them was Ulianov, Lenin's eldest brother. While the baleful influence of the fanatical reactionist, Pobyedonostzev, was felt in every department of public affairs, there were some capable ministers in the cabinet of Alexander III. Among them were Sergei Witte, under secretary of 

State, Professor Vyshnegradsky, economist, and Prince Khilkov, who as a young man gained experience in Pennsylvania mines and now served as administrator of railroads and highways. Through their efforts, certain economic reforms were instituted, which materially lessened the depression. Thus a bank was established to aid in the rehabilitation of the destitute nobility. Another bank was organized to take care of the needs of the peasants. Certain labor legislation was also enacted by which exploitation of employees by employers was somewhat limited. The outstanding material accomplishment of this period was the construction of the Trans-Siberian Railway, decided upon in 1885, and actually begun from both Vladivostok and 

Cheliabinsk in 1891.

Under Alexander III, Russia, for the most part, stood aloof from European affairs and ceased to interfere in the concerns of other nations. This policy of "hands off" had come increasingly into favor following the Congress of Berlin in 1878. The Emperor trusted no foreign power, had an especial hatred for England, and tolerated no outside meddling as a return for his own non-interference. During his reign, Russia but once had resort to arms. This was the conflict on the Kushk 

River with the Afghans in 1885. At its conclusion Russia came into possession of the "Merv and Pendeh Oasis."

Although Alexander III had robust health and was noted for his extraor dinary strength, he died in 1894 at the comparatively early age of 54. He was succeeded by his son, Nicholas II.

RUSSIAN LITERATURE IN THE 19th CENTURY 

If we had no history whatsoever of Russia during the 19th century, we could nevertheless reconstruct most of it from the literature of the period, inasmuch as this literature was closely interwoven with current events. It was in the truest sense a living literature, and this we must bear in mind, if we are to understand it completely. Under the censorship of a great part of the period there was, as previously indicated, little freedom of the press or freedom of speech. Writers, however, were permitted comparative liberty in fiction and through the various literary mediums voiced with much freedom opinions which, under a less rigid censorship, would have found expression in the field of journalism. 

Their productions are remarkable for the absence of the slightest trace of commercialism. Each author wrote what he wished with perfect independence, and without the least pretense of catering to the taste of the public or to demands of publishers. With few exceptions, these independent spirits came from the ranks of the Russian nobility. This does not mean necessarily the nobility by right of birth, but more often the nobility by right of intellect. It must be evident, therefore, that conditions in Russia in the 19th century were favorable to the production of the very highest type of literature. It occasions no surprise, therefore, that its message is universal, although it deals with local problems. Because of this universality, an appreciation of literature in general would be incomplete without an understanding of the Russian classics of the 19th century.

When we speak of Russian classical literature we mean the literature of the 19th century, and more especially that which flourished in the Golden Age under 

Alexander II (1855-1881), the Abraham Lincoln of Russia, The Golden Age has as its extreme limits, however, the poet, Pushkin, who began his work under Nicholas I, and Leo Tolstoy, who ended his career in the reign of Nicholas II. Before Pushkin, Russian literature was confined almost exclusively to translations, adaptations, and imitations of foreign productions. During the Golden Age, it became a genuine outgrowth of Russian soil and Russian life.

From the beginning, Russian classical literature suffered because of the early demise of its creators. A few statistics will give eloquent testimony of the brief span of life enjoyed by some of Russia's most brilliant literary geniuses. 

Thus Pushkin lived from 1799 to 1837; Koltzov, from 1808 to 1842. Lermontov, who is called the "Russian Byron" and the "poet of eternity," was born in 1814 and died in 1841. Then there was Belinsky, the "Russian Lessing," 1810 to 1848, and also

Griboyedov, 1795-1829. In considering the tragic departure of these men at a period in life when many a brilliant mind has just begun to exhibit its power, one is tempted to wonder, in view of the magnificence of their accomplishments, what colossal heights they might have attained had they been granted longer years. 

We shall now give a brief summary of the accomplishments of certain of the literary lights of the 19th century, referring only to those, who in our opinion, best reflect the ideas and ideals of the period. There is no question that the literary progenitor was A. S. Pushkin, the "Shakespeare of Russia," who laid the foundation for future accomplishments in the field of Russian letters. He transmitted to others the inspiration and ambition to continue that which he himself was not able to complete in his short life. His influence is clearly discernible throughout the entire period, many ideas being directly traceable to him. A Russian 71

poet in the most perfect sense of the word, he greatly increased the vocabulary of his native tongue, and established standards in literature which served as models for all time. It was during his time that Russian began to supplant German and French among the upper classes. His Evgeni Onegin, written in the years 

1823 to 1831, was a novel in verse, which became the pattern for all subsequent novelists in Russia. His Boris Godunov was the first serious attempt at creating 

Russian drama. Although the poet Lermontov surpassed all other Russian poets, Pushkin excelled him in reaching the hearts of the people and stirring their emotions. His poetry reflects completely the tendency toward Russification of the age of Nicholas I. 

Another dramatist was Griboyedov, noted especially for his ability at characterization. In his Misfortune of Being Clever, staged 1833, he did a remarkable piece of work in the character Tchatsky, who typifies a "Decembrist." 

With Gogol (1809-1852) began the Russian novel. Before him, practically everything produced was in verse, but his novels inaugurated the age of prose, wherein lies the glory of Russian literature in contrast to English literature, which finds its highest expression in poetry. Gogol's works depict realistically the life of the Ukrainian peasant, past and present with a hint of the future. 

Taras Bulba (1836), which has been called "a poem in prose," is a tale of the Cossacks of the Ukraine during the 14th and 15th centuries. It is sometimes referred to as the Russian Iliad. Gogol, however, did not confine himself solely to portrayals of life in this region but also dealt with various phases of Russian life in general. His Dead Souls (1842) is a faithful picture of the wretched life of the serfs and the overbearing domination of the land owning nobility. It came as a great revelation to the Russian people, to whom the life of the serf was for the most part a dark mystery. Dead Souls is the first genuine novel written in the 

Russian language, and its appearance marks the dawn of Russian literature in prose. 

The comedy The Revisor (1836-1842) is an amusing but keen satire on provincial officialdom. While it reflects clearly the seamy side of public life, and was much opposed by the bureaucrats, it was the reactionary Nicholas I, who brought about its publication. Upon the occasion of its first performance, he was in the audience and was the first to applaud. 

Following in Gogol's footsteps was Gontcharov (1812-1891). Of his three novels, Oblomov, a portrayal of the landed nobility, which appeared in 1858, is regarded as a classic. In a sense, he may be considered as a forerunner of Turgenev (1818-1883). 

The heart of the Slavophile-Westernizer controversy of the 19th century finds literary expression in the writings of Turgenev and Dostoyevsky. The works of the former have an especial bearing on the age and the reforms of Alexander II. In 

Memoirs of a Sportsman (1852), Turgenev supplemented the work begun by Gogol in Dead Souls. Whereas the latter novel revealed for the first time the external conditions of Russian serfs, Turgenev revealed the soul of the serf and presented him as a human being. Memoirs is apparently merely a series of harmless sketches, but by reason of its extreme objectivity, it was in a great degree responsible for the abolition of serfdom. In 1862 appeared Fathers and Children, which is probably the most widely discussed and the most misunderstood book in Russian literature. 

With the exception of Dostoyevsky, not a single literary man seemed to have grasped the real meaning of the novel. The character of Bazarov, the nihilist, is a prototype of the

Communist-Bolshevik of 1917 to 1921, and Turgenev intended his novel to be a warning to rulers of Russia to take cognizance of the appearance of this type. Instead of taking it as a warning, however, they thought that he was preaching nihilism and condemned him and his novel accordingly. Dostoyevsky, however, who understood its message, began where Turgenev stopped, and in his Possessed produced a sequel to Fathers and Children. For this reason these two novels should always be read together. Turgenev, after he had created Bazarov, and allowed him to voice his opinions, shrank from his own creation when the time came for the nihilist to act and caused him to die instead. 

Dostoyevsky, in his immortal novel which crowned him as the prophet of Russia, ¨: showed the Bazarov type in action. Whereas Turgenev presented a truthful picture of the character and mode of thought of the nihilist, Dostoyevsky showed to what lengths of violence and absurdity such a type might go when acting in accordance with his principles. Certainly only the "possessed" could act after the fashion of 

Dostoyevsky's characters.

Of tremendous importance in its bearing upon social problems is Turgenev's Virgin Soil (1876). In this the last of his novels--he wrote seven in all--he offered a definite remedy for Russia's social and economic troubles. He showed this not by his main character, Nezhdanov, but by a secondary character, Salomin, a middle class type. Thus early Turgenev called attention to a class, whose importance and significance for the welfare of the country was not generally recognized until the days of Stolypin. It took Turgenev himself twenty years to discover this type, which offers so striking a contrast to the impractical idealist of Rudin (1856). 

Dostoyevsky (1821-1881) pictured the Russian people at their best and at their worst. His work may be defined as the science of the Russian spirit. Furthermore, to know him is to grasp the enigma of the Slavic race and to solve the Russian puzzle. He may be also called the General Booth of Russia's insulted and injured. 

For an understanding of Russia today, therefore, his writings are indispensable. 

This is especially true of his four major novels: Crime and Punishment (1866); 

The Idiot (1869); The Possessed (1873), and The Brothers Karamazov (1880). Aside from the vivid and truthful delineation which these novels give of Russian life with its varying ideas and ideals, they have also a universal significance, and may be regarded as almost indispensable for a study of criminology, psychopathology and theology. The scientific world has, indeed, taken cognizance of Dostoyevsky's treatment of the first two of these branches of knowledge, and excerpts from his novels have even been inserted in text books on the subjects. The third field still remains to be investigated.

If Dostoyevsky takes rank as the deepest thinker in the field of Russian letters, without doubt the most popular is Leo Tolstoy (1828-1910). One of the earliest, as well as one of the latest, of 19th century writers, he stands forth as the gigantic figure of the period. With him the classic age of Russian literature comes to an end. Physically of the West, spiritually of the East, he worked as if with the brush of a painter. His greatest sympathies were for the peasants, the simplicity of whose life he delighted to portray. His outstanding productions are: War and Peace (1865-1869); Anna Karenina (1878); and Resurrection (1899). A work of an earlier period which has had a unique influence is his Childhood (1852), Boyhood (1854), and Youth (1857). A Russian writer, Pisarev, has made it the basis for a new system of pedagogy which is a distinct Russian contribution to that field. In addition Tolstoy's fame has been increased by several works which appeared posthumously.

100

Tchekhov (1860-1904) began as a writer of short stories and ended as a playwright. His dramas mark a distinct transition, reflecting the modern trend of thought which formed the background of the revolution of 1905. Beginning with Turgenev, the majority of the writers had idealized peasant life. As Tchekhov saw it, however, Russian retrogression was due in great part to too much peasantry. His dramas picture all the dullness of village life, wherein both the peasant and the idle rich vegetate in bored stupidity. The message of two of his dramas, in particular, Three Sisters (1900) and The Cherry Orchard (1904), may be interpreted as a call for a return to the city.

Gorky (1869-1936) goes even further and openly resents the over-idealization of the peasantry. He prefers instead to deal with the worker in the city. He pointed out that much misery and wretchedness may be traced to peasant influence, and championed "urbanization versus ruralism." On this account he became, aside from his radicalism, the classic author of the Soviet regime. 

His best known play, the Lower Depths, 1902, procured for him a popularity without precedent, especially in Germany. Here during the season 1903-1904, it was played five hundred times in succession. If Turgenev discovered the soul of the serf, in this drama Gorky discovered the soul of the dwellers in Russia's underworld. 

These beings differ sharply from underworld characters in America or in any other country. They are the victims of a social order, and under other conditions would never have sunk so low.

In Leonid Andreyev (1871-1919), author of twenty-eight plays, voiced the sentiment of the Russian intelligentsia, that was awakened by modern progress. his dramas he questioned the fundamentals of our life. He became a problem writer, challenging God, Satan, Destiny, Reason, and Life. His best known play is

Anathema (1909). 

A dramatist, whose plays are still much in vogue is A. N. Ostrovsky (1823-1886). He was at his best in portraying the life of the Russian "Babbit" of his day. Of his 54 plays, the best known are: It's a Family Affair We'll 

Settle it Ourselves (1850); Poverty is no Crime (1854); A Protege of the Mistress (1859); The Storm (1860), and Sin and Sorrow are Common to All (1863). It was due to his efforts also that the Russian theatre was established upon a sound basis, thus paving the way for the founding of the Moscow Art Theatre in 1897. 

In the field of music, we shall merely mention the names of a few whose contributions have become the common property of the Russian people by reason of their adaptation of Russian folklore and melodies. Freeminent among them as a national musician is Glinka (1804-1857), who did for Russian music what Pushkin did for Russian literature. To the following also is iue the highest honor for the beauty, nobility and vital importance of their contributions: P. I. Tchaikovsky (1834-1887); M. P. Mousorgsky (1839-1881); A. P. Borodin (1834-1887); N. A. Rimsky 

Korsakov (1844-1908); and S. V. Rachmaninov (1873-1945). 

Note:

For further information on Russian literature during this period, see 

The Golden Age of Russian Literature by Ivar Spector (Revised and Enlarged Edition, The Caxton Press, 1946).

THE LAST OF THE ROMANOVS (NICHOLAS II, 1894-1917) 1. Reign of Emperor Nicholas II (1894-1917). 

Nicholas II came to the throne on November 1, 1894, and was crowned in Moscow on May 14, 1896. He lacked both the temperament and the training necessary for an able ruler. His unyielding obstinacy alternated with a weak vacillation. 

At times he was very susceptible to influence, but almost invariably seemed to yield to that which was most pernicious. While fairly well versed in the field of general literature, he had only the most inadequate and incorrect knowledge of the science of government. Like the majority of Russian princes and grand dukes, he had received an early education which had served to implant in him unreasoning and stubborn prejudices toward certain races and individuals. 

In November, 1894, he married a German princess of the house of Hesse, who accepted the Orthodox faith, and assumed the name of Alexandra Fyodorovna. From the first the new Empress was very unpopular. Even her wedding day was darkened by a horrible public tragedy, which earned for her thus early the title of the "funeral bride."* Because of her lack of Russian sympathies and her inability to adapt herself to Russian life, she was constantly the object of suspicion and distrust. The people resented the fact that she refused to speak Russian unless obliged to do so, and made English and French the preferred languages at court. 

Her influence over Nicholas II was believed to be great, hence she received the blame for many reactionary measures. It is true that she was even more prejudiced and narrow-minded than he, and upon occasion, prevented him from making much-needed concessions. However, on his own responsiblity Nicholas made it extremely plain shortly after his accession that he intended to follow the policy of his father. 

This he did by tightening of goverment control and censorship in all fields where independent thought or action might be suspected of making an appearance. 

Alexander III had succeeded in re-establishing firmly autocratic rule, which had been somewhat weakened by the reforms of Alexander II. He was able to do this because he very carefully kept out of foreign entanglements, and thus avoided blunders abroad, which would weaken his prestige and authority at home. On the other hand, Nicholas II permitted himself to become involved in foreign affairs to his and to the country's exceeding disadvantage. The failure of his foreign policy was largely responsible for the social disorders which marred his reign. 

Following the Congress of Berlin in 1878, the relations between Russia and England continually became more strained, for by her constant expansion in Central Asia, the former was drawing nearer and nearer to India. For reasons of her own, Germany aided Russia in maintaining her hold on her possessions in the Middle East. 

Nevertheless, Alexander III had a profound distrust of anything in the nature of an alliance with Germany, since he was fully aware that the latter's "beau geste" was *Thousands of spectators, who were waiting for the royal cortege, suddenly broke through the police lines. A panic ensued, in which some 2,000 persons were crushed and trampled to death. In spite of this disaster which plunged the city into mourning, the usual state ball was held in the evening at the French Consulate. 

This was a great blunder on the part of the sovereigns and contributed much to their unpopularity. 75

VOAND 2009

motivated solely by self-interest. However, the isolation in which Russia had found herself after 1878 made it extremely necessary that she secure an ally. Even more than an ally, she needed a loan. France had become the abode of the financiers of that time, hence it was natural that Russia should seek financial assistance in that direction. Previous to 1880, she had depended chiefly on German banks, but in that year as the reign of Alexander II was drawing to a close, she was able to negotiate a loan of considerable size with French bankers. Henceforth up to the year 1906, she sought and obtained large sums of money from this source. This financial connection led to a political rapprochement, * and in 1895, a Franco- Russian Entente was brought about through the efforts of Ribot, Prime Minister of France.

Despite these financial and political relations with France, Nicholas II realized that Russia was by no means in a secure position. In addition to being constantly pressed for money, he had an army, which was but poorly equipped as compared with the military forces of his European neighbors. In consideration of the se facts, he took the initiative in calling for a conference of the Great Powers in the interests of international peace. The first Peace Conference convened at The Hague in 1899. In 1907, when Russia was much weakened by the Japanese War and the Revolution of 1905, the second Hague Peace Conference was called. Although the second 

Conference assembled at the invitation of Nicholas II, the suggestion for it came from President Theodore Roosevelt. Neither conference succeeded in bringing about any practical and lasting adjustments. The Great Powers were much too distrustful and jealous of one another to render any understanding possible. England resented the Franco-Russian alliance, Germany was wary of England, and Russia was suspicious of all of them.

While international peace was of supreme importance to all Europe, no country stood in greater need of it than Russia. Even before the first Hague Conference in 1899, a group of farsighted statesmen, under the leadership of Witte, had seriously urged the abandonment, at least temporarily, of the policy of territorial expansion in favor of an economic expansion in the markets of the Middle and Far East. This, they believed, would contribute materially toward the establishment of internal stability, but for the attainment of this end, there must be guarantees of external peace. A step in this direction was the understanding with France, which was much to the interest of both countries in view of Germany's growing militarism. 

Germany, for her part, maintained peace with Russia and rendered her rather valuable assistance in European politics, because it was to her own interests to do so. 

She particularly favored Russia's activities in the Middle East for two reasons. Her own objectives were in the Near East, in Turkey. If Russia were occupied further afield, she would not meddle with Turkey, and at the same time would draw away the attention of England from the region in question. Russia's pretensions in the Far East were, at that time, of no particular interest to any nation but Japan, and hence were not as yet a factor to be considered in European politics. 

The mutual interests of Russia and Austria in the Balkans alone threatened to lead to immediate hostilities in the course of adjustment. To be sure, Alexander III had *A French squadron visited Kronstadt on July 25, 1891. The ceremonies attending this visit made a great impression upon Russian society. Most incongruous was the order by the autocratic Tsar that the revolutionary "Marseillaise" should be played.
